Besides the aforementioned Wei Dai, the American computer scientist Nick Szabo
was one of the first people to be involved with digital contracts and digital currency.
In 1998 he already designed a mechanism for decentralized digital currency what he
called ‘Bit Gold’. Bit Gold was never implemented as Bitcoin was at the beginning
of 2009. However, Bit Gold is seen as the direct predecessor of Bitcoin and there is
also speculation that Nick Szabo Satoshi is Nakamoto, but he denies that69. However,
he has indicated that there are many similarities between Bit Gold and Bitcoin.
So far, a more detailed explanation of the creation of digital money and Bitcoin and
the building blocks that serves as basis for blockchain technology. Now we continue
with a more detailed explanation of the Bitcoin blockchain. As stated previously,
there are several (types of) blockchains. The Bitcoin Core (BTC) blockchain is one
of them.
3.2 The Bitcoin blockchain
As explained before, the story of Bitcoin started when Satoshi Nakamoto sent a message to the Cryptography Mailing List. The message was sent on 31 October 2008,
with as subject: ‘Bitcoin P2P e-cash paper’. This message included a link70 to a pdf
(white paper). Figure 3.1 shows a copy of this message71.

69 See for example article by Martin O’Leary in The Huffington Post: ‘The Mysterious Disappear-

ance of Satoshi Nakamoto, Founder & Creator of Bitcoin’ (5-5-2016).
70 See also website: bitcoin.org/bitcoin.pdf
71 For a list with e-mails and postings, see also the: Satoshi Nakamoto Institute.

Boek8_Kopie_Intern_Gebruik_Weconomics

79

Figure 3.1: Message from Satoshi Nakamoto to a cypherpunk forum

On 9 January 2009 Nakamoto sent the message that the first bitcoin was released
and invited others to participate in the network:
“If you can keep a node running that accepts incoming connections, you'll really be helping the
network a lot. Port 8333 on your firewall needs to be open to receive incoming connections.”
When we as authors are invited for a keynote for instance, we are often asked to
name specific examples of blockchain applications for a certain industry, for a certain
context, for example the recruitment industry, financial sector or government domain. But blockchain basically doesn’t see any context. Blockchain is industry agnostic you could say. For a blockchain it doesn’t matter if the transaction concerns a
bitcoin, hundred bitcoin, this book, a house or a 13.7 billion aircraft carrier. However,
as soon as we start to use Blockchain Organizing from the paradigm of a certain
domain, we immediately miss the essence of Nakamoto’s intention. In one of his
blogs Nakamoto already wrote that he wants to be able to accept every form of value
and every transaction (not just bitcoin): “…The nature of Bitcoin is such that once version
0.1 was released, the core design was set in stone for the rest of its lifetime. Because of that, I wanted
to design it to support every possible transaction type I could think of. The problem was,
each thing required special support code and data fields whether it was used or not, and only covered
one special case at a time. It would have been an explosion of special cases. The solution was script,
which generalizes the problem so transacting parties can describe their transaction as a predicate that
the node network evaluates. The nodes only need to understand the transaction to the extent of
evaluating whether the sender's72 conditions are met.”
72 Please note, just like with the Weconomics model, Nakamoto puts the sender in the centre (in

the sense of: initiator of a transaction). So, the individual who makes the transaction, for example
ordering a book at Bol.com, is at the centre and not the order procedure of Bol.com.

Boek8_Kopie_Intern_Gebruik_Weconomics

80

Bitcoin as money
Bitcoin is a number in an accounting system (ledger) what at some point represents
a certain value (has a number) a certain trust. This value, this trust, is only beneficial
to you if other participants in your community will do the same as you do. The numbers get practical value if someone wants to exchange bitcoins for bread, a car or a
dollar and believes that others will act accordingly. Cryptographic digital money, like
bitcoin, will only become a generally accepted medium of exchange (currency) if others trust that they as well can use it as a medium of exchange, as a claim on future
value exchanges. Bitcoins and numbers in a ledger only have value, like the euro and
the dollar, because you and many others believe that they have value. When the trust
in a coin or a number in an accounting system disappears, you cannot exchange the
value that is recorded in the books. When the trust is reduced, the medium of exchange will lose its value. Bitcoin is currently not supported by a government or a
central bank, but by a protocol that ensures that a certain amount of bitcoins can
come into circulation. It is supported by a community with members who believe
that the value of bitcoins will remain constant or increase. This of course attracts
speculators and in this respect, it does not differ from regulated currency. We also
speculate with the dollar and euro.
However fiduciary currencies (fiat currencies; see also table 3.1: The Money Matrix
of the ECB), such as the dollar or euro, are indeed supported by central banks. There
are regulations and instruments such as monetary policy, including quantitative easing for example, to maintain or change inflation and the value of, or confidence in,
the euro or dollar. In general you can say that money, whether it is digital or not, has
the purpose to reduce the insecurity when transactions take place. The higher the
trust level is, the lower the insecurity. But besides the advantage that money offers
as a medium of exchange, money also has other functions that often involve disadvantages such as accumulation, charging interest, derivatives and speculation. Fiduciary currencies, like the euro, are no longer supported by real values such as gold or
the gross domestic product. For more information about money and the role of
money in our economy and society we refer to the Weconomics analysis book, paragraph 5.4: ‘Economy and the role of money’.
In order to send and receive bitcoins you install a software program on your computer. Bitcoin Core is the original program but now there are also variants at the client
side. The software program contains a wallet, what is actually an asymmetrical key
pair. The client software is some kind of key holder with which you can ‘sign’ your
transactions from your wallet. The private key (call it a pin code) provides access to
the wallet, the public key (call it your account number) is the address of the wallet. In
between there is also a digital signature (call it digital fingerprint). A digital signature
works by using these two different keys that are connected to each other: the private
key for creating the signature and a public key for having the transaction checked by
others. You can see the private key as a real password, the signature as proof that you
have the password without having to reveal the identity of the sender.
Wallet software

Boek8_Kopie_Intern_Gebruik_Weconomics

81

Not everyone with a wallet needs to assist in the maintenance of the network. A
(Bitcoin) wallet has two asymmetric keys: a public key and a private key. A wallet is a
piece of software that you can download, say on your smartphone. Using this software you can make payments and check your balance. Wallet software sends a transaction from the sender to the receiver. Subsequently this transaction is (if it complies
with the rules) included in the blockchain during the next acceptance moment. Acceptance is done by miners. You can compare wallet software with an App for internet banking. In the core it is client software to send and receive payments and to
check the balance. There are several providers of wallet software. After you have
downloaded the wallet software, you can also create Bitcoin addresses of your friends.
You can compare this with the address book used in internet banking or your e-mail
address book. The sending and receiving of bitcoins is similar to sending and receiving e-mails. Paying and receiving Bitcoins is even possible by holding two phones
together with NFC technology73. The ‘sent to’ address can be scanned via a QR code.
In figure 3.2 you can see an example of Bitcoin wallet:

Figure 3.2: Example of a Bitcoin wallet

A distinction can be made between offline wallets (for example on your PC), that can
be used to download the complete database, and online wallet software (also called
eWallet). With the latter, you do not have to download any software but you are
dependent on the supplier. You do not have the wallet in your hands and when the
supplier goes bankrupt or has bad intentions, you can lose your bitcoins.
Mining
Mining is finding consensus on the state of a distributed ledger. Nodes verify transactions (keys, signatures, input references and double spending) and find either consensus on it or not. Mining is also a way to arrange transactions in a certain chronological order in a distributed system (sequencing). A central database (of a bank for
instance) has no problem with sequencing: first come first served. Sequencing on a
distributed network is necessary because not all nodes receive a transaction at the
same chronological time. To accept a transaction, you must wrap it in a block so to
73 NFC, Near Field Communication: contactless communication which uses a NFC chip and has

a range of about ten centimetres. This technology can be seen as an extension of the RFID technology (Radio Frequency Identification).

Boek8_Kopie_Intern_Gebruik_Weconomics

82

say, whereby, with a hash, a reference to the previous block is made, which creates a
‘fixed’ chain (blockchain). Strict and rigorous rules are applicable to this, based on
mathematical proof. Mining is some kind of competitive lottery whereby you, as individual, almost never can guess the winning ticket. Not even when you buy a lot of
tickets. Therefore, an individual node can never gain control over the network and
for instance, issue an input reference twice. When a block is included in the longest
(chain) branch a miner earns bitcoins (Proof of Work). Therefore, other miners check
the offered block if everything is correct. In addition, they always check if they have
the longest chain. If this is not the case, they automatically switch to the longest chain.
The protocol that ensures that only a limited number of bitcoins can be mined, mainly
applies to rewarding the miners. It does not say a lot about the value of the bitcoin.
After all, a bitcoin is programmable money that you can divide and exchange via
exchange markets. A total of 21 million bitcoins can be made. It is estimated that the
final bitcoin will be brought into circulation somewhere in the year 2140. You can
divide each bitcoin again in 100,000,000 units (also called Satoshi’s). Bitcoins can be
divided up to 8 decimal places (0.000 000 01) and possibly even smaller units if this
might be required in the future when the average transaction size reduces. It is not
known what the value is or will be of this smallest part. This value is usually determined on exchange markets. You can compare these with (border) exchange offices.
Mining is like searching for bitcoins by solving a puzzle. The person who solves the
puzzle first, receives a reward that gets smaller and smaller (in numbers). The reward
is halved after every 210,000 mined blocks. Mining bitcoins, solving the puzzle, was
initially done with a processor (CPU). The level of difficulty of the puzzles to be
solved was much lower then. Nowadays mining is more and more done using the
graphic card of your computer. Special cards have been developed for mining such
as the ASIC (Application Specific Integrated Circuit). During mining not only a puzzle is solved but the transactions are also verified.
If you install bitcoin software to verify transactions, all transactions up to and including the first transaction, are downloaded on your computer. They are not just downloaded but also verified. Please remember that you execute transactions on a blockchain with strangers that you cannot trust. You must be able to check every transaction yourself in the copy of the ledger on your computer. Downloading and verifying
the software can take up to twenty-four hours. But fortunately, you only have to do
this once. On January 1st 2016, the entire file was: 53.7 GB, on January 1st 2017: 96.3
GB and on January 1st 2018: 149.3 GB.

Bitcoin network

Boek8_Kopie_Intern_Gebruik_Weconomics

83

As soon as a new node is added to the network, it is provided with some kind of
address list to make contact with the other nodes74. These become the 'nearest neighbors'. Later on, new addresses are ‘discovered’ from this list and added to the address
list. You could compare this with LinkedIn. You start with the people that you know
and have met personally (tier 1) and from tier 1 you start to build a tier 2 of people
that you do not know personally (yet), etcetera. The address list of the Bitcoin network is expanded to create an unstructured peer-to-peer network, in which each
node ‘communicates’ with a large number of other nodes. Communication is done
in such a way (with mathematical substantiation and proof) that this makes the network sufficiently secure.
In addition to the protocol that is used to create transaction messages and to verify
transactions and blocks, there must also be a ‘system’ to develop, offer, accept and
process software for example. There is discussion about the size of the block for
example. Not all Bitcoin nodes follow the same rules.
It is important to which layer (stack) a rule applies and to what extent this rule influences other layers So, following different rules does not have to be a problem, as
long as the entire system functions as intended. Not all developers will follow the
same rules when implementing the rules. However, developments within Bitcoin
Core75 require community-consensus before adjustments to the rules can be implemented. This is important because other application layers depend on this. An example of such a rule is the size of the block. Within the Bitcoin network we can
distinguish four layers:
1. Core with consensus rules about the level of difficulty of the puzzle that must be
solved with the mining.
2. Peer-to-peer: these rules determine how full nodes share data and which data they
share. It contains rules related to sending and receiving transactions, blocks and
special data packages.
3. Application Programmable Interface (API) and Remote Procedure Call (RPC):
this is a communication layer between the peer-to-peer infrastructure and the application. For example, Bitcoin wallets and block explorers communicate with the
network via this layer.
4. Application (wallet): contains rules about formats for addresses, generating private
key and making backups of your wallet.
The consensus rules of the core are the most important rules of the Bitcoin network.
They organize a number of important matters such as the block size and block
See also article in Bitcoin Magazine by Aaron van Wirdum: ‘Why Some Changes to Bitcoin
Require Consensus: Bitcoin’s 4 Layers’ (26-2-2016).
75 Bitcoin Core (formally Bitcoin-Qt) is currently the third Bitcoin client and co-developed by
Wladimir van der Laan (from Eindhoven, The Netherlands) and based on the original work and
the code of Satoshi Nakamoto. Wladimir is the successor of Gavin Andresen who resigned in
2014 as core maintainer. Andresen was the successor of Nakamoto.
74

Boek8_Kopie_Intern_Gebruik_Weconomics

84

reward, the level of difficulty for solving the puzzle (hash rate) and the type of Proof
of Work that is required. When all nodes follow the same consensus rules, it guarantees that the same copy of a blockchain is maintained. When nodes accept other
consensus rules, they could also accept other blocks that have been rejected by other
nodes. This could create different versions of the same accounting system. As a consequence it does not provide a shared reality and opens the way to fraud.
From Bitcoin to other blockchains
So, we now understand the blockchain concept specifically in relation to Bitcoin and
its underlying blockchain technology. Bitcoin is a form of crypto currency, a form of
value that no longer includes physical or digital money but software code or programmable money. The Bitcoin Core community has no central authority like the
European Central Bank (ECB) but it is a form of distributed authority with a shared
reality in assets and transactions. Core of Bitcoin, the underlying blockchain technology, as well as Blockchain Organizing, is the transaction between sender and receiver,
using coordinating capacity (S → C → D). Hereby value flows, via a transaction,
from supply to demand. Algorithms and encryption is used to make the connection
meaningful, secure and reliable. In fact, the coordinating capacity between demand
and supply is programmed.
Bitcoin is regularly in the news, in a positive sense but often also in a negative sense.
Just as it would not have been smart to assess the value of internet solely based on
e-mail as a replacement of the letter, it will not be smart to judge the power of blockchain technology based on Bitcoin only. But in our opinion, it would be a good development when more people invest time in creating blockchain communities instead of spending time on the speculation what the Bitcoin price will do. Instead of
creating jobs in trading, or worse speculating on something that has to be developed,
it’s better to invest time in the design and development itselves.
Bitcoin is one of the many manifestations of blockchain technology and certainly of
Blockchain Organizing. Bitcoin is proof that blockchain technology can work longterm, is stable and useful. Bitcoin shows that blockchain technology technically
works well and cannot be easily adjusted involuntarily (at acceptable costs). To evaluate if blockchain can do something for your organization it is important not only to
appraise Bitcoin but also to study the underlying blockchain technology. To assess
Blockchain Organizing it is important to look at the underlying organizational model
of the Weconomics Foundation.
So far, a more detailed explanation of the Bitcoin (BTC) blockchain. Now we continue with an explanation of the different types of blockchains that are currently
available.

Boek8_Kopie_Intern_Gebruik_Weconomics

85

3.3 Different types of blockchains
Many innovations and their practical applications are similar to their predecessors.
The first television broadcasts were mainly a copy of the radio broadcasts (for example: radio plays or classical concerts with images). The underlying technology was of
course completely different. To embrace new technology it is smart that it is similar
to what we are used to. For example, the first websites were mainly copies of paper
brochures. A skeuomorph is something that seems functional because it looks like
something that we already know. Most use cases or Proof of Concept projects we
see in our consultancy practice come from existing organizations and contain existing
processes. And therein lies a great danger. Blockchain is not business as usual, disguised
as innovation. Blockchain is not meant to digitize existing processes. It is not a change
within a system. It is mainly a change of the system. The system that especially will
change is the firm as superior form to organize work and transactions. But when you
invest too long and too much in a new technology within an existing system it will
lead a life of its own. This happened with the development of internal communication systems based on the TCP/IP protocol in the nineties.
Because old thinking models and interests prove to be persistent, this currently leads
to the development of all kinds of flavors of blockchain. We see an explosion of use
cases and everybody wants to be first and unique and this is where it is already going
wrong. Digital and blockchain are industry agnostic. A blockchain doesn’t care which
value is in ‘the box’ that it transport from A tot B.
Basically, there are two extremes: a variant that is closest to the current way of organizing (permissioned blockchain) and a variant that is the furthest away (permissionless). We mainly believe in the form in between: blockchain for communities or
business-ecosystems. Although blockchain has a lot of standardization in ‘itselves’,
there is no standard yet, like the TCP/IP protocol for internet. Time will obviously
tell which flavors will remain, whether or not there will be a good (good for the
people) standard and how this will look like. Till then it is important to understand
the thoughts and drivers behind these various flavors and developments.
We, as authors of this book, are not neutral when it comes to what flavor a blockchain should have or get. We combine blockchain technology with a, although for
many new, but existing and proven organizational model of the Weconomics Foundation. We believe that the principles of Weconomics correspond to the principles
as described in the white paper of Nakamoto. But Weconomics is not value-free. It
is a community that wants to use organizational technology to create a surplus to
make our welfare more sustainable. You the reader, might not want sustainable welfare but only more welfare? The Weconomics Foundation uses certain values, intents, perspectives, visions and ambitions. So, we also supply a certain blockchain
flavor, but we try to ‘cook’ with widely supported organizational principles as much
as possible and to stay as close as possible to the original organizational principles
that we believe are meant by Nakamoto and his/her predecessors.

Boek8_Kopie_Intern_Gebruik_Weconomics

86

According to us, Blockchain Organizing can be best applied within partnerships,
ecosystems, smart networks, shared transaction networks, also called communities.
It makes little sense when a bank, insurance company, government or webshop starts
its own private blockchain. Then we must exchange values between blockchains. In
essence Blockchain Organizing is meant for peer-to-peer transactions within value
chains and communities. Based on this insight you can distinguish the following
blockchains:
▪ B4B: Blockchain For Business (permissioned)
▪ B4P: Blockchain For People (permissionless)
▪ B4C: Blockchain for Communities (hybrid)
An important difference in flavor is the difference between private (permissioned)
and public (permissionless) blockchains. We believe that combinations of these two
extremes are also possible. Usually we call these combinations: hybrid blockchains.
We see these three flavors on the internet as well: open internet, login internet and
intranet. According to us, there are essentially two main flavors: a decentral and a
central flavor. The central flavor (top-down) is mainly fed by existing organizations
with old DNA, old conditionings and the idea that we require certain institutions to
organize trust. The decentral flavor (bottom-up) is expressed in the original idea from
Nakamoto. Central or old thinking is not necessarily wrong, but when you continue
too long with old thinking in a new (digital) context, things could go wrong. We have
seen this with companies like Eastman Kodak or Blockbuster. They probably went
bankrupt because they continued too long with old thinking in an environment that
digitizes more and more. The tragedy with Eastman Kodak is that they invented the
change that eventually killed it.
It seems almost inevitable, but our constant tendency towards centralism, which we
can also see in the Bitcoin blockchain, property and shielding is widely present,
driven by evolution and it is difficult to stop. It goes back to our ancient survival
instinct. After all, you have collected something or found a fertile plot of land. This
gives you freedom, welfare and a higher survival rate and you want to secure this.
Everything that subsequently affects the status quo, you rather want to exclude. But
it is like Leon Megginson76 formulated it:
“It is not the strongest of the species that survives, nor the most intelligent,
but rather the one most adaptable to change.”
You usually have gone to great lengths to acquire or experience what you currently
possess. Therefore, we will defend the existing with all we have against ‘robbers’,
competitors or people who want to share. After all, much energy and money were
spent on collecting the existing. Of course, you will not give that up easily. The whole
76 Leon Megginson, professor at the Louisiana State University tried to summarize Darwin’s cen-

tral idea in the book ‘On the Origin of Species’, during a speech for the Southwestern Social Science Association and came to this formulation (1963).

Boek8_Kopie_Intern_Gebruik_Weconomics

87

idea of ‘my property’ and of ‘wanting to control’ is the foundation for this. Because
if you have more (goods, knowledge, food) than someone else, theoretically you have
a higher survival rate. But apart from this pattern, we are also social animals and our
survival rate and standard of living increases when we cooperate. This might surprise
you, but our ancient instincts and dispositions still play a role, also in the development of blockchain. Maybe they play an even more important role than many might
expect or hope for. At this moment in any case you’ll see that the technique does not
immediately change the behavior of people (speculation, opportunistic behavior, etcetera) regarding for example Initial Coin Offerings (ICO) and the development of
crypto futures.
Before we further discuss the two main flavors of blockchain (B4B and B4P) and the
hybrid form (B4C), first an addition to the question: ‘What is actually the difference
between central, decentral and distributed?’ We will explain the difference between
these three forms in more detail in the following paragraph.
Central, decentral and distributed
As indicated before, blockchain technology can be used for maintaining a distributed
ledger (divided across the participating nodes). However, when a ledger is technically
distributed, it does not necessarily mean that power is also distributed. Everyone can
buy bitcoins, but not everyone can open a bank account. A blockchain may be distributed in infrastructural terms, just like internet, but that does not mean that the
power is also distributed. A widely used picture to display the difference between
central, decentral and distributed networks is the depiction by Paul Baran77. With his
research, Paul Baran contributed to the design of internet. He suggested to make the
communication network robust, (for example the failure of the network due to a
nuclear attack), by working with packages that would be transported from sender to
receiver via different nodes. At the receiver end, the different packages would ‘merge’
again. This principle is the basis for internet and therefore also for blockchain and is
called ‘packet switching’, or package-switching (see also the TCP/IP protocol in paragraph 3.1).

77 Paul Baran (1926-2011), originally a Polish engineer, but moved to the US later on. He studied

packet-switched networks (Asynchronous Transfer Method) and worked for Rand Corporation,
among others.

Boek8_Kopie_Intern_Gebruik_Weconomics

88

Figure 3.3: Paul Baran: difference between central, decentral and distributed network structure
from infrastructural perspective

In figure 3.3 there are three different variants. But actually, there are four if you include the governance aspect, in addition to the technical aspect.
Four different forms
An extension of figure 3.3 does not only depict the technical aspect, but also the
chosen governance model. As mentioned before, in addition to technology, especially the chosen organizational model and, within this the governance perspective,
is important. What are old interests and legacies and how do these emerge in the new
design? With a central protocol you can still have a lot of power in a peer-to-peer
network. Examples are business platforms like Airbnb and Uber. Another and more
comprehensive picture than figure 3.3, can be found in the blog of the company
Maidsafe78. This also includes the governance aspect, in addition to the technical
aspect. We then have four variants:
1. (Fully) central
2. Distributed and within:
3. Decentral and within:
4. Peer-to-peer (fully decentral).

78 See article by author Ioptio: ‘Evolving Terminology with Evolved Technology: Decentralized

versus Distributed’ (4-12-2015).

Boek8_Kopie_Intern_Gebruik_Weconomics

89

Figure 3.4: Four different networks from infrastructural and governance perspective

In Figure 3.4 you immediately recognize the difference between infrastructure and
‘being in control’. In other words: a distributed network (infrastructural) can indeed
be controlled centrally (governance). An example of this is the SETI project79,
whereby thousand computers release a piece of computing power but are indeed
controlled centrally. An important example of a decentralized blockchain, ‘without’
central stakeholder and with a peer-to-peer infrastructure, is the current Bitcoin network. Naturally you can call Nakamoto, who could mine the first bitcoin easily and
therefore probably became ‘rich’, a stakeholder. But at this moment anyone who
wants to join, can. Nobody will be excluded by a central authority. Whether you also
have the opportunity and can invest enough to mine bitcoins is another story. But in
the design, nobody and everyone has ‘the power’. Thousands of computers (nodes)
assist to validate the transactions and to forward these to other nodes.
These are two important features of a technically distributed situation: validating and
forwarding without exclusion. The so-called full nodes, guarantee the decentralized
character of the network. For example, there must be ‘consensus’ in more than fifty
percent of the entire network. Consensus about the validity of a transactions. There
is not one node that can determine the sequence and what the others should do. The
‘democratic law’ of half + 1 applies in this system. If more than fifty percent of the
79 See also website: seti.org

Boek8_Kopie_Intern_Gebruik_Weconomics

90

participating nodes agree with a transaction, this will also be valid and that will be the
new shared reality. The new valid transaction is ‘added’ to the already existing shared
reality (accounting system). The design is based on the fact that it would be impossible that one single node or party can solely achieve this majority. That this eventually
could be different in the practice of mining pools, Nakamoto could probably not
have imagined (or maybe he/she/they did?).
The Bitcoin network also tends towards a certain form of centralization because only
a limited number of mining pools mine the most Bitcoins. This tendency towards
centralization is a very natural process because it appeals to our natural tendency to
scale up (becoming more and more efficient). But eventually it is about the balance
between central (scaling up, mechanical organizing) and decentral (flexible, organic
organizing). More about this central-decentral balance in the Weconomics theory
book.
To be allowed to add a block to the blockchain in the Bitcoin protocol, it must be
validated as well as ‘mined’. Basically, validating means an answer to the question
whether or not a transaction may be executed. So, the transaction is compared to the
agreed rules that state that there must be an input reference before a value can be
issued and if this is the case, that this value subsequently cannot be issued more than
once. To get a better understanding why it is so important that there is a difference
between decentral and distributed, the following is important. Mining has two purposes:
▪ guaranteeing the (technical) security of the network;
▪ an economic boost.
The latter means that there has to be a motivation or a temptation, so people want to
perform work for others (checking the transactions, for example currently done by
the accountant). This performance of work and the proving thereof, is called Proof
of Work. Often it is said that a transaction within the Bitcoin network does not cost
anything; that is not completely true. It costs energy of course and to grant a transaction priority it is wise to use a so-called ‘miner’s fee’, otherwise you have to wait a
long time before your transaction is ‘approved’. A very small amount to pay the miner
for the performed work. This amount, however, is still a lot lower than the transaction costs in the traditional monetary system. If you do not offer a miner’s fee with
a Bitcoin transfer, which is allowed, the crediting of the transaction will take longer.
In addition to the miner’s fee there is also another type of remuneration. As soon as
a miner can prove that he has performed work (has correctly solved the puzzle), he
receives a certain number of bitcoins.
From these three to four network variants, we can derive two different blockchains
as extremes on a scale and a more hybrid form in between.

Boek8_Kopie_Intern_Gebruik_Weconomics

91

Public, permissioned and hybrid blockchains
As explained before there are basically two flavors of blockchains and a combination
of both (hybrid) forms. The two flavors are centrally (dictatorial, authoritarian) controlled and decentral (consensus). These are the extremes on the scale, whereby many
variants are possible between these extremes. An example of this is a democratic
decision: half + 1. In terms of infrastructure there is elimination of intermediary entities or processes in all cases. This will certainly bring economic benefits from a
procedural point of view. After all, ‘agents’ are removed and therefore the friction is
reduced (unless the energy bill becomes too expensive). For example, clearing and
settlement processes in the financial world, whereby it is possible that there are five
parties between the provider and receiver, between the initial seller (cash machine)
and end consumers (invoice shopkeeper).
We saw a similar layout, centralized/decentralized, when internet emerged. In the
beginning, communication applications were built that (in terms of infrastructure)
worked with the TCP/IP protocol and therefore infrastructural decentralized but
still controlled centrally (intranet). This situation still exists: the communication department, for example, can determine who has access to the network and who has
not and who can contribute, edit, delete, etcetera, and who cannot. Now we see a
similar development again. Currently many blockchain developments are taking place
within companies (central control), but with decentral blockchain technology. We
call this permissioned blockchains as opposed to open, public or permissionless
blockchains whereby there is ‘no’ central authority.
In principle, the infrastructural border is not relevant for Blockchain Organizing. We
reason from the smallest building block and you as an organizational participant are
part and total (holistic). A company, a city, a country: for Blockchain Organizing
these are all just (collections of) connections between the smallest building blocks.
Power and authority are all the more important to Blockchain Organizing. We want
to get rid of all kinds of intermediaries to organize trust. We want to get rid of central
powers of large companies and governments. We want to return the power to the
people.
The following table gives a good overview of the difference between permissionless
and permissioned blockchains:

Table 3.2: Differences between permissioned and permissionless blockchains 80

Public blockchains or permissionless blockchains
The public blockchain with Proof of Work (necessity for mining nodes), is currently
the only ‘fully’ decentralized blockchain. After all, it is a public and transparent blockchain, with open source software, whereby every participant can and may send transactions to everyone. Nobody is owner. Consensus is proper consensus because each
participating active node has the same ‘vote’. There is no exclusion. The control of
power lies with the majority of controlling miners. No external control can be exercised on, for example, the Bitcoin community or accounting system. There is a reason
why governments, financial institutions and large companies are suspicious of this
type of blockchain: they cannot exert any influence (at least not without adjusting

80 Copied from the blog by Yacine Ghalim.

Boek8_Kopie_Intern_Gebruik_Weconomics

93

existing laws and legislation). The most important public blockchain, proven in practice, is currently the Bitcoin blockchain.
Private or permissioned blockchains
As soon as a blockchain is fully controlled by one party or organization, it is a private
blockchain. This is an example of old thinking; after all it is based on the (legal) entity
the firm, the bank or the government and not on organizing capacity. This situation
does not require any or other types of ‘mining nodes’ then the current one. In any
case, one does not have to be afraid of the so-called 51% attack, as the control is
100% within the own organization. The important question with private blockchains
remains: why would you apply expensive database technology within your own company when there are cheaper alternatives such as a central database? We feel that
some companies believe that they are a ship and by attaching wings they can turn it
into an aircraft, while the ship is much too heavy to take off.
The consequence of private blockchains will be comparable to private communication systems such as Microsoft Outlook. Sharing information with the environment
then becomes the sending and retyping of e-mails. Sharing transaction data will soon
become the retyping of blockchain A to blockchain B. Analogue processes are digitized, but the way in which that demand and supply or trust is organized, changes
little to nothing81. It is our opinion that private blockchains have little to do with the
original idea behind Bitcoin. Private blockchain owners must first have the courage
to give up some autonomy, let go of the existing and conditioned management model
(the firm) and need to think more in terms of communities and shared transaction
networks. Only then does the use of blockchain technology make sense. If we do
not do this, the blockchain becomes the next internet which will provide little benefit
to us, human beings. A typical statement to illustrate this is a statement from the
Chamber of Commerce82: “The complex matter of blockchain can largely automate the administrative processes of the Chamber of Commerce, but the Chamber of Commerce will not be redundant
for a long time.”
A derived form of a permissioned blockchain is a consortium blockchain. We see
this for example happening at banks and insurers. In the financial sector the idea has
probably emerged to separate the term blockchain from the term Bitcoin. Because
Bitcoin creates a serious threat while they can apply blockchain technology ‘inhouse’. The consensus mechanism with consortia blockchains is vested in a number
of privileged participants. The right of access and crediting is determined by the consortium and therefore by definition there is exclusion.

81 See for example also article Nikolai Hampton in Computerworld: ‘Understanding the block-

chain hype: Why much of it is nothing more than snake oil and spin. Blockchains are an exciting
new development, but the devil is in the detail’ (5-9-2016).
82 See also report (edited) by Marlous Plomp and Koen Hartog: ‘Results Blockchain pilots’ (June November 2016), see also website: https://www.blockchainpilots.nl/home-eng

Boek8_Kopie_Intern_Gebruik_Weconomics

94

Incidentally, a partial right of access can be provided to the public as a result of, for
example, the new privacy law and then you could speak of a hybrid situation or partial
decentralization. But the control or power clearly lies with the consortium. This does
not require any or other types of mining nodes. After all, the trust lies within the
consortium itself. However you look at it, when you reason from the smallest building block, a company with CEO is also some kind of community whereby certain
governance rules apply. A CEO cannot make all decisions and depends on statutes,
management and a Supervisory Board. You cannot simply say what is central/decentral or permissioned/permissionless without also mentioning the unit of analysis and
design. And the most important thing remains: with what purpose do I design organizing capacity?
Bitcoin versus blockchain
The idea that the only useable part of the Bitcoin ecosystem would be the blockchain
technology and that you can just separate this from the entire ecosystem, is a bad
one. This bad idea is subsequently positively framed by the existing establishment.
Circumstances are created that make this bad idea come true. But only removing the
blockchain technology (whatever that exactly might be) from a large total (Bitcoin
ecosystem) is like trying to let a polar bear survive in the desert. It would also be a
bad idea to apply Blockchain Organizing within the walls of existing companies.
However, what could be useful is to prepare existing internal processes with the use
of Blockchain Organizing, so that these can be transferred to a shared transaction
network.
The disruptive nature of Blockchain Organizing mainly lies in the fact that with pure
decentralization (infrastructure and governance are decentral) there is a serious knock
on the door of the existing social-economic establishment. An establishment in
which we still organize trust with incumbents, with companies, banks, governments
and political parties. At this moment, the establishment seems mainly interested in
the ‘underlying technology of the Bitcoin’. But if we had applied the assembly-line
within the crafts workshop a hundred years ago, we would not have become so prosperous.
Apart from technology, leadership and the management model must also change at
the same time and that will take more time, attention, money and resistance than only
changing the technology. The question is, whether just changing the technology, or
for example the medium, is actually progress? ‘And what is progress? When does
technology provides progress and who does actually progress?’ Yacine Ghalim83 says
about this: “As much as I don’t think one type of blockchain is inherently better than the other,
I certainly believe that permissionless blockchains are far more disruptive and transformative. In the
eyes of many the blockchain is seen as this disruptive piece of technology while Bitcoin is portrayed
as a Napster-like experiment. I don’t think this is the right way to look at it.”

83 See also blog by Yacine Ghalim: ‘Why we should drop the whole “Bitcoin vs blockchain” dis-

cussion’ (7-10-2015).

Boek8_Kopie_Intern_Gebruik_Weconomics

95

Although blockchain technology can also provide efficiency improvement within
companies (a company is also in fact a collection of smallest building blocks), the
technology will mainly become disruptive when it is applied in shared transaction
networks where not one company is or can be ‘the boss’. We would find it strange if
each company would design and apply its own Windows or Linux operating system.
However, you can develop different applications that work on an operating system.
This means that we first have to organize supply and demand differently (management model), then we have to develop a joint infrastructure (data common, ontology,
taxonomy), then a connection to operating systems (for example Ethereum based)
and after that each company, but also each citizen, may develop his own blockchain
or application (DAPP: Decentralize Application). This means that we can all build
our own garden path but that the road network becomes a commonality. Because
why should we do this for the road network and utilities and not for the data facility?
As a result of all this, there are only two flavors left for us that can make the difference in how we can support a completely new way of organizing capacity with organizational technology: the permissionless or hybrid blockchain. Only these setups
ensure that we can organize trust without increasing bureaucracy, complexity and the
power of large companies and central government. How this will develop will mainly
depend on the setup and existing institutions (top-down) in relation to the success
of start-ups (bottom-up), which are able to apply blockchain technology in shared
transaction networks.
Now we will discuss a number of (technical) blockchain concepts and tools that we
can use within Blockchain Organizing.
3.4 Blockchain concepts and tools
In this paragraph we will give a further explanation of a number of tools and concepts that are part of, and contribute to, Blockchain Organizing. Together, these
tools offer the opportunity to bring supply and demand together in a decentral and
democratic way without significant friction costs, while simultaneously reducing insecurity in an ever more digitizing society. We will limit ourselves to a number of
technical concepts and tools for Blockchain Organizing:
▪ Data Logistics
▪ Data As A Service
▪ Personal Data Service
▪ Vendor Relationship Management
▪ Attribute Based Encryption
▪ Distributed Systems
▪ Smart contracts
▪ Decentralized Autonomous Organizations
▪ Ethereum
Data Logistics

Boek8_Kopie_Intern_Gebruik_Weconomics

96

People and tools generate more and more data: the house we live in, the car we drive,
the sport we practice, the holiday destinations we visit, the goods we buy, the job
sites we visit, the educational courses we book and our contacts with government.
Even our body generates increasingly more data. The smarter the device and the
more we do online as a person, the more data we generate. And all this data must be
stored, preferably in an efficient way, and converted into useful information.
Opinions differ on usefulness and necessity. What remains is that only the most efficient tool that brings demand and supply of data together, will eventually survive.
It is our belief and it is a law of nature. Water, air and electricity: they all seek the way
of least resistance to go from A to B. An important tool to provide data to the buyer,
at the right place, at the right time and in the right form, is Data Logistics 84. Data
Logistics is also called information logistics, but information is derived from data.
As explained before, Blockchain Organizing, in its core, is: bringing supply and demand of added value together, with as little friction as possible. Bringing demand for
data (information) together with supply of data (attributes). Logistical concepts can
ensure that this is done in a quick, smart, affordable, safe, meaningful and reliable
way. With the production, transport, storage and repacking of data (bits and bytes)85
and the consumption of data (information), we can draw parallels with the physical
world of production and distribution logistics. Logistics for bits and bytes is called
data logistics in order to distinguish it from the ‘normal’ logistics related physical
goods.
Logistics is mainly concerned with the production of goods from: raw material (producer) to end product (consumer). Logistics or broader supply chain management is
the flow of goods from source to customer. Data logistics is about the transport,
storage and repacking of data, bringing data to the next part of the chain or to get
information. This can be between a single buyer and a supplier but there can also be
logical preceding and follow-up processes in a chain. Basically, the Bitcoin protocol
consists of a number of dependent processes such as finding other nodes, creating a
private and public key, a digital signature, sending a transaction message in the network and subsequently the hashing, mining of transactions in order to realize a block
and eventually a blockchain. In the figure below we show an example of data logistics
regarding transport, storage and repacking of data between three suppliers and three
buyers:

84 More about data logistics in the Weconomics analysis book, paragraph 3.5: ‘Weconomics com-

munication’.
85 Bits and bytes basically consist of two distinct voltage or current levels allowed by a circuit (or
it may be the two stable states of a flip-flop, two positions of an electrical switch, two distinct levels of light intensity, two directions of magnetization or polarization) and are generally not seen
as a physical product or material with a specific weight and size.

Boek8_Kopie_Intern_Gebruik_Weconomics

97

Figure 3.5: Data logistics, transport, storage and repacking

Data As A Service
Data As A Service (DAAS) is a concept whereby data is offered from a service. Bring
Your Own Data (see also Personal Data Service) is part of this. In this case, you as
consumer/citizen/employee provide the necessary data that organizations require to
deliver products/services to you. The organizations do no longer have to own data
themselves, only ‘access to’ is required. DAAS is derived from an already existing
concept called Software As A Service (SAAS). Hereby organizations do not have to
install software themselves on for example their computers, they can use software in
the cloud. DAAS assumes that data, needed to perform an organizational process,
can be provided when this is required. The data can be provided by smart contract,
blockchain technologies, data logistics and quick connections to one source file. This
does not mean that physically there is one file or database, but in definition (in a
logical sense).
There is one definition and with the use of a protocol attributes from different and
decentralized databases are retrieved. This does not just improve the quality of the
data. The hacking of data is also becoming more and more unattractive. Historically,
organizations store data in their own database or repository86. This was partly due to
the physical condition of the first databases. Records went missing due to physical
defects, power failures or software errors. That is why in the seventies and eighties,
86 Literally: receptacle, reservoir, container or storage space.

Boek8_Kopie_Intern_Gebruik_Weconomics

98

most organizations started to manage a database with a backup service in order to be
able to restore lost records. But the quality of databases has improved enormously
and the storage and transport (internet connection) costs have been reduced rapidly.
This means that we can move to another and more distributed concept of data storage: from database to DAAS with the cloud as interim solution.
A database, or more specifically a data structure, model or description, is an important part of the blockchain infrastructure as we already saw in paragraph 3.1. Traditionally (the sixties), a database is a physical archive (similar to a filing cabinet),
usually at one location, to record, store, consult and delete digital data. The new invention (database) resembles the old functionality (filing cabinet). Attributes were
usually connected to a unique key, such as the personnel number, national insurance
number or bank account number. This way a record of attributes was created that
were connected for example to an employee, citizen or tax payer.
Initially records were stored on tapes and later on disks and hard drives. But as indicated before, the physical storage is more and more separated from the description
or data model. Another aspect, not related to the physical way of storing, is the relationship between data and the process for which the data is used. To appreciate this
you need a bit of historical development. In the early days of automation (from the
middle of the last century), mainly sub-processes were automated. By taking separate
processes the automation became less difficult. But if subsequently these separate
processes are not correctly aligned, a situation is created that becomes increasingly
complex (combination of super specialization and bad alignment, see also trust paradox in paragraph 1.1). Due to a bad coordination between specialized processes the
total does not become more productive.
The solution for this problem is sought in a joint database per company or function:
for example an ERP, CRM and HR-systems87. So, in this stage, data is separated from
sub-processes. The ideal situation, storing all data in one database, is not possible,
for example because of political or privacy reasons. This creates solutions such as
data warehousing and the development of interfaces such as webservices and APIs.
Since then more and more automation companies are working on the development
of conversion and migration software to unlock various databases. In addition, the
functional needs with sub-processes increase and subsequently the applications must
be adjusted again per sub-process and therefore also the interfaces. This creates increasingly more complex IT systems that few people will understand, and therefor
increases the risk of failure and data leaks. This is still happening at company level.
We see this with banks that still run old mainframe systems or with executive organizations such as the Tax office and the Social Insurance bank.
From the nineties, the need increases to also perform processes together with the
(business) environment. For example, the use of a procurement system by the
ERP: Enterprise Resource Planning, CRM = Customer Relation Management, HRM = Human Relationship Management.
87

Boek8_Kopie_Intern_Gebruik_Weconomics

99

outsourcer, to which a supplier must connect. Fortunately, the solutions are also increasing, especially internet offered solutions. The ‘website-webserver architecture’
is actually a copy of the ‘client-server architecture’ (terminal-mainframe). The recourses change but the fundamental way of organizing between data-supply and
data-demand does not change substantially. The company remains the starting point
for automation, data models and setup of a database.
Blockchain will also have little effect as medium, as tool, if we do not change the
organizational design and take data and individuals as the starting points rather than
companies and sub-processes. By continuing to choose for internal solutions, in a
world with increasingly more outside connections, new (integration) processes and
complexity are created. As long as the internal process is leading over available data,
the data is still organized per company. The solution is sought in webservices and
APIs, that subsequently also increase in numbers so they are more and more difficult
to control. Here we see it again: by reducing insecurity, the insecurity increases. This
is not because of the technology but because of applying new technology in an old
management model. The firm is still the unit of thinking, analysis and design when
automating. The question is if Blockchain Organizing, or for example the new European privacy legislation, can break this cycle, this paradigm.
Personal Data Service
Personal Data Service (PDS), Personal Data Management (PDM) and Bring Your
Own Data (similar to Bring Your Own Device, BYOD) are part of a wider and
aforementioned concept: Data As A Service.
PDS is the possibility to keep your own data and to give organizations and other
natural persons only permission to use your data for the purpose that they must
specify in advance: for example the organization of a project or the processing of an
order. For instance, a webshop may only use your address details for sending a product, after that these details may no longer be present in their system for example to
be resold, whether or not anonymous, to data brokers or advertising companies, unless you give permission yourself. You can also see PDS as a digital bank with safes.
Each safe contains an attribute; a characteristic that belongs to you. For example,
‘street name’ or ‘date of birth’. In these safes, you can securely store your personal
data. You can send other people a key to access a certain safe. The key (target group)
comes with an access code (purpose): you give someone the key to your house but
with a specific purpose: only to water the plants. You need another key to get to your
jewelry.
PDS is made up of open and free-source software, an agreement system and protocols for autonomous implementation. Combined with internet and blockchain technology they make a secure, distributed and shared data network possible. A network
of various actors such as users and associated organizations that conduct transactions
with each other and agree to handle personal data in a responsible manner. For example, requesting personal data by a webshop can be seen as a transaction that can
take place on a blockchain. Apart from using blockchain technology, PDS can be

Boek8_Kopie_Intern_Gebruik_Weconomics

100

connected to a general data utility facility. This is a kind of road network or power
network, for personal data and (value) profiles. Examples of PDS applications (context- or domain-driven) are:
▪ Electronic88 Portfolio (e-portfolio): structured overview of work and leaning experiences with connected values89 such as: training courses, tasks, projects, employers, positions, assessments.
▪ Electronic Medical Record (EMR) and Personal Health Record (PHR): structured
overview of values connected to a person in the role of patient. The PHR is seen
more as a file that belongs to the person and the EMR as a file that belongs to the
care provider and of which there are several per individual, in case a person uses
several care providers. Actually, both files are also resources connected to a person
(day hospital bed, medication X, treatment Y, doctor conversation Z etcetera).
▪ Electronic Financial Record (EFR): structured overview of connected financial
products with connected values such as: a credit, an insurance policy, a pension,
shares and bonds. At this moment, there is hardly any cooperation in this area
between seller and buyers (consumers and companies)90. The purpose of the EFR
is to provide people and organization insight into various financial products, via
one application, which may or may not belong to different sellers or buyers.
▪ Consumer record: structured overview of values connected to a person in the role
of consumer. For example, an overview of all products and services purchased
online. You could make the metadata from this overview available (sell) to advertisers or use it for benchmarking and reputation management in a community that
works with blockchain technology.
▪ Citizen Record (CR): structured overview of values connected to a person in the
role of citizen. For example, permits that you request, an allowance that you receive, an application for a new passport or the payment of taxes.
Via a PDS, various organizations such as insurers, training institutes, temporary employment agencies, governments, banks and webshops can offer their products and
services via your own channel. Your own channel means that you have an own website or app, to find services and products and to conduct transactions. When this
website is connected to a general data utility facility you can easily let the ‘access to
data’ flow between seller and buyer whereby the buyer only needs to maintain data
at one location and the buyer can search, book and conduct (blockchain) transactions
with his own application. An organization may only use your data to perform an
internal process, for example the processing of a permit request. Via PDS you are
assured (with mathematical proof) that companies and institutions can only use your
88 Most applications start with an ‘e-’ from electronic and mainly illustrate the transition from pa-

per to digital. When electronic has become self-evident (and paper is seen as not normal) the ‘e’
before the name of a record could better be cancelled.
89 With records it implies that connected values can also have a status such as: planned, approved,
signed up, confirmed, evaluated, invoiced, paid, registered, archived.
90 Most people store their (insurance) policies in paper form in their archives. In the Netherlands
the Dutch Association for Insures opened a website in 2018 where customers can access their
policies digital, independent form the insurance company. This project already started in 2015.

Boek8_Kopie_Intern_Gebruik_Weconomics

101

data for the purpose for which you have granted access. It is expected that the market
for PDS services will rapidly develop and grow. The Boston Consulting Group91
estimates the value of the European market for Personal Data in 2020 to become
1,000 billion euros. Personal data is a potential large market that is expected to grow
by 20% every year.
PDS and General Data Protection Regulation (GDPR)
To prevent that organizations use your data without your permission PDS is an important addition to the stricter European legislation via for example GDPR. But only
legislation is not enough and it provides a false security. Existing institutions like
governments, banks and companies, will quickly go back to solutions within the same
frame of mind, more institutions such as: rules, supervision, IT and more employment. These adjustments will not affect the right to exist of the institutions that offer
these institutions but mainly strengthen it. But the question is not what institutions
want, the question is what is best for the people and our welfare. There are better
solutions from the human perspective.
PDS is an important new concept for this that can be built with blockchain technology. PDS is a cloud-based system to securely store your data, to make it accessible to
or to share it with other persons who may use your attributes to get an answer to a
question. It seems that GDPR will become the main guideline for the protection of
personal data and thus the design of IT systems for the coming decades. GDPR will
have a great influence with this, not just on IT companies and IT organizations but
also on all organizations that record personal data in HRM, CRM or ERP systems,
for example. We believe that it is important to know more about the new privacy law
in relation to PDS. To prepare yourself now will prevent high fines and costs for
adjustment later (for example the problems regarding the millennium bug). In addition, it also offers new opportunities for individuals and organizations that indeed
organize correctly, from the design.
Not property but access is relevant
In order to comply with the new privacy law, to become more productive and to be
less dependent on existing online platforms, there is a growing belief that not the
property of data is relevant, but the access thereto. If an organization does not own
any data it cannot leak this data. In a knowledge-intensive society much data is connected to knowledge workers who increasingly work for several clients. We see a
change in the models used to organize work: from company, via cooperation in a
business eco-system, to a network community with shared transaction networks. In
this community, there is smart cooperation with customers, suppliers, employees,
governments and other stakeholders. When we continue to cooperate with the firm
as starting point and unity of analyses, IT systems become more and more complex.
Plus, the number of (internet) connections with the company’s environment
91 See report of the Boston Consulting Group: 'The Value of Our Digital Identity' (2012).

Boek8_Kopie_Intern_Gebruik_Weconomics

102

increases and the risk of data leaks continues to increase as well. To solve this problem, we often go back to more security or more use of IT and lawyers. This traditional way of thinking does not solve the problem but rather makes it worse. It is
better to look at Privacy by Design. The use of Blockchain Organizing can help with
this.
PDS on a blockchain
With PDS you cannot just assure your privacy and the value of your personal data
better, but you can also become more productive. PDS is the possibility to maintain
your data yourself at one location. PDS can be connected, for example, to Personal
Data Search (non-manipulative search engine) and Personal Transaction Services.
These create their own blockchain (for example e-portfolio) where there is consensus
by the community (that a transaction has taken place) while you can still indicate
yourself who can access your personal data. Only part of the data is decentralized on
each node (distributed ledger, on-chain, for example: sender, receiver, timestamp,
amount and key to off-chain). Using a unique key you can retrieve additional attributes from a PDS (off-chain). When you connect transactions (processes, workflows)
that are automatically conducted, to your own records, your Personal Health Record
(PHR) or your e-portfolio, using a smart contract for instance, updates become automatically reliable, secure and meaningful. Examples are: purchasing medication
from a pharmacist or subscribing to a course with a training institute.
With PDS, you only allow e-commerce companies to use your data for the processing
of an order. Or you allow a trainer to use your e-portfolio for the composition of a
course group, but when the event has finished the data may no longer be available
to the trainer. Eventually, PDS ensures that your personal data no longer roams the
internet, but is stored in your own safe location and is only available when you allow
this. Blockchain Organizing can help to design and realize this.
PDS uses the blockchain principle, whereby humans and resource are the two smallest building blocks. On the supply side (S) these two are connected to a specialization,
which, whether or not with the use of coordinating capacity (C), are connected to a
demand or finalization (D). This creates two transactions (resource to human as a
specialist and a specialist to finalization). Transactions are created and can be executed and registered via Blockchain Organizing principles. A resource (value) is
transferred from seller to buyer. The required data for his process can be retrieved
from a PDS. A PDS can be connected to a peer-to-peer platform that works with
blockchain principles. One of these principles is that the sender must prove ownership of this value and singular spending capacity. Subsequently the value flows from
sender to receiver. This is not so much about the flow of a physical product (book)
for example from author to reader), but about the associated product information
(price, author, dimensions). This data also represents a certain value that flows from
sender to receiver.

Boek8_Kopie_Intern_Gebruik_Weconomics

103

PDS with Blockchain Organizing starts with creating a profile (personal data and
resources). Subsequently the buyer can find a product or service and make a purchase
and payment. A peer-to-peer platform brings buyers and sellers directly into contact
with each other, as much as possible, for example: consultancy services or training
courses. It makes transactions possible without having to trust each other and without being able to spend a value twice. As mentioned before the most important processes involved are: profiling, selecting, purchasing and payment.

Figure 3.6: Processes related to the preparation, implementation and registration of transactions

In more detail, this means paying attention to the following concepts:
1. Personal Data Services (PDS) whereby users (sellers) can maintain their profile
and can decide who is allowed to join.
2. Personal Search Services (PSS): this concept offers the possibility of Non-Manipulative Search92 whereby not the advertiser (seller) determines what you find, but
the user (buyer).
3. Personal Purchase Service (POS, O=Order): this concept offers the possibility
that the seller and buyer jointly set up the workflow consisting of: approval, confirmation, delivery and registration.
4. Personal Payment Services (PPS): this concept offers the possibility that the seller
and buyer jointly select the payment system. For example, with bitcoins but also
via an own community token.
Vendor Relationship Management (VRM)
The way to organize demand and supply is still largely determined by the producer,
despite the development of internet. You must always enter the same data on various
websites if you want to order something. Products and services are ‘pushed up’ the
market through sales. The question is: can Blockchain Organizing can provide a reversal of the supply and demand model. Internet has not sufficiently managed this
yet. Many ‘Big Data’ investments also do not contribute to the reversal of the model.
They mainly focus on using data to promote sales. Can Blockchain Organizing
92 Many search engines are manipulative and use for example advertising banners at the top.

Boek8_Kopie_Intern_Gebruik_Weconomics

104

ensure that we switch from a push to a pull economy? One of the concepts that can
help with this is Vendor Relationship Management (VRM).
VRM and CRM
You can consider Vendor Relationship Management as the counterpart of CRM
(Customer Relationship Management). CRM is often supported by a CRM system.
This is usually an IT system with data and characteristics of:
1. suspects (leads, Market Qualified Leads: MQL)
2. prospects (promising leads, Sales Qualified Leads: SQL)
3. customers
The purpose of a CRM system is to take parties from suspect to customer as quickly
and cheaply as possible. The purpose of many organizations is to lock-in third parties
(other organizations and consumers). Systems for communication, sharing information and making transactions are currently often still driven by the supplier and
not by the buyer. Blockchain Organizing, and embedded therein VRM and PDS, are
aimed on reversing this: the buyer has an own portal (pull instead of push) to find
and book products and services and to make transactions.
VRM is aimed at Customer Driven Leads (CDL): giving a prospect the tools to commit to you and to become a customer when there is a need. A seller must enable a
buyer to find his product (information) when the buyer needs this product. And
when the buyer has found this product, a transaction must be enabled at minimum
transaction costs and without lock-in, using buyer’s own data to perform the process.
We expect that push tools, such as direct-marketing, will be used less due to VRM
systems. Therefore, the advertising revenues could be structurally reduced in the
coming years and decades to come, undermining the business model of many social
media platforms. With the increasing power of large data companies, and for example
the problem of fake news, we believe that more people will connect to a data utility.
With this they can convert the present data into relevant information, at the moment
they require it. VRM can be seen as a new dance, a tango whereby the seller no longer
leads, but the buyer does. Despite empty cries like 'putting the customer first', most
organizations still use their own CRM system and not a VRM system. Essentially,
there does not have to be a technical difference between these two systems. There is
mainly a difference in perspective on how we can organize trust, work and economy
with the less friction an the highest standard of living per person. How do we organize supply and demand, using these techniques, enabling the buyer to be in control
and not the seller? A situation where transactions can be made against much lower
costs, with less complexity and with much lower waste as a consequence.
What is VRM and the ProjectVRM?
The term VRM was first used in an article of ComputerWorld magazine in May 2000,
but with another meaning. The meaning of VRM we use, was first used in a

Boek8_Kopie_Intern_Gebruik_Weconomics

105

conversation93 between Mike Vizard and Doc Searls94. Vizard saw VRM as counterpart of CRM. The conversation was about an initiative, the ProjectVRM, which Doc
Searls had just started. The project mainly focused on developing tools and services,
but from a new perspective on CRM. CRM involves high transaction costs such as:
branding, marketing, targeting, capturing, acquiring, lock-in and managing. With
VRM, customers are more like partners who want to connect against much lower
transaction costs and therefore a lower end price. According to ProjectVRM the
main theory behind VRM is: 95: “Many market problems (including the widespread belief that
customer lock-in is a 'best practice'), can only be solved from the customer side: by making the
customer a fully empowered actor in the marketplace, rather than one whose power in many cases is
dependent on exclusive relationships with vendors, by coerced agreement provided entirely by those
vendors.”
VRM ‘inventor’, Doc Searls is, among others, the author of the book ‘Intention Economy: When Customers Take Charge’. According to Searls it is not about the seller but
about the buyer. Since the internet was ‘released’ in 1995 we have seen that internet
is a tool that can provide the buyer more ‘power’. However, not a lot has actually
changed since then in the fundamental way we organize trust and consumer interaction. The solution for lower transaction costs cannot only come from the technique.
Several powers are at play here that we want to make meaningful within Blockchain
Organizing in relation to finding a solution to improve productivity, privacy and balance of power.
In 2012 Customer Common96 was created from the Project VRM with as purpose:
education, research, support and the development of VRM tools for individuals
when they get or have a relationship with organizations. VRM is also part of a large
development that we usually call Life Management Platform. These are platforms
that enable people to control their own data in relation to organizations that need
this data to perform processes. This concerns data from all types of domains, for
example data from: your body, home, car, work, education, purchases, finances and
your relationship with the government.
PDS and Vendor Relationship Management (VRM) want to achieve the same thing:
more direction and control by individuals in relation to organizations that use, process and sometimes even resell data. PDS and VRM reason more from humans or
individuals while existing CRM and HRM systems reason from the tool humans creates such as companies, banks or government authorities. You could see VRM as

93 Podcast from Doc Searls with Mike Vizard (1-9-2006) about a project that Searls started as fel-

low at the Berkman Centre for Internet & Society at Harvard University.
94 Doc Searls (1947-), American journalist, open-source promotor, fellow Center for Information
Technology & Society (CITS) at the University of California, Santa Barbara, and an alumnus fellow (2006–2010) of the Berkman Center for Internet & Society at Harvard University.

counterpart (but also addition) of the traditional CRM system that is more based on
a push-market than a pull-market97.
Attribute Based Encryption (ABE)
More and more companies discover the convenience of cloud services to store data.
The use of cloud services offers many advantages such as: scalability, flexibility, accessibility, ease of use and low costs. But ‘the cloud’ also has concerns that cannot
always be covered legally and therefore require technical measures. Because many
cloud services (for example Dropbox and Google Drive) were initially primarily used
by private individuals, relatively less attention was paid in the design to the security
of data. The use of own resources, the so-called Bring Your Own Device98 concept
and the increasing number of cyber-attacks, require extra attention to security and
encryption. Encryption is used to guarantee the reliability and integrity of data, but it
can also be used for access control. When for example patient data or personal data
is put in the cloud, you want to be able to offer proper access security that goes
beyond the existing authorization systems with a login and password. You want to
be able to connect certain rights to a role that has access to certain attributes of a
person. These rights are based on a certain policy or on certain instructions or conditions. Only the role, with associated rights, that complies with the instructions or
the protocol can access a certain range of attributes. Plus, it should not be necessary
to give access to the value of an attribute (for example date of birth). It must be
sufficient to get a correct answer to a clearly formulated question: may I send person
X a ballot paper because this person has turned eighteen today or is older?
When you assume that you cannot trust a cloud server, you must add more security
at data level. You can do this by encrypting attributes, in such a way that the owner
of the data can determine via a key who (which role) gets access to which attribute
or date (range) and for which purpose (rights). This is similar to placing data in a safe,
whereby you send a key to the receiver. So, we see many similarities between the
ABE concept and the Bitcoin protocol with key and digital signature. You could say
the blockchain technology uses ABE technology. You could also say that ABE uses
blockchain technology whereby you see the request for information (are you eighteen
today or older) as value exchange, as a transaction, that can be run via a blockchain.
ABE is an important security concept that can be used with almost every role-based
system that must offer trust and integrity. A role-based system means that you connect certain rights or policies to the role of ‘manager’. These policies indicate what
the role can and cannot do. The advantage of this system is that it does not depend
on the person who fulfils a role. Persons can change jobs, but roles usually last longer.
A role-based system is also called Role Based Access Control (RBAC). Cloud services
often work within a federative environment. This means that you for example can
use your Facebook account to log on to your cloud service (Single Sign On). To
97 See also the Weconomics practise book, paragraph 3.5.6: ‘Communication matrix’.
98 See also the Weconomics practice book, paragraph 5.4.1: ‘Bring your own device (BYO)’

Boek8_Kopie_Intern_Gebruik_Weconomics

107

improve the security within federative environments, policies are often based on attributes. We call this Attribute Based Access Control (ABAC).
ABE goes one step further. Within ABE a user also has a set of attributes (such as
age, or country of origin) in addition to an ID (for example Social Security Number).
Parties that want to access the data of users must have a correct combination of ID
and attributes. For example, you can program that certain IP addresses or GPS coordinates do not get access. Values that are outside the normal user pattern can also
be excluded. When a user always requests access during the day and suddenly once
at night, this could mean that someone from a different time zone is trying to log on.
By logging the behavior of users, patterns are created. A login that differs from this
pattern can for example be excluded. Concrete applications of ABE can be found in
initiatives of Harvard and MIT: Sieve and Enigma99.
Distributed Technology
Distributed Technology is a collective name for a number of concepts and technologies that are used by Blockchain Organizing. In figure 3.4 we saw four different
types of networks: centralized, distributed and within this decentralized and within
this peer-to-peer. With a centralized system, all users (clients) are connected to one
central server. This server records the time and the server contains one database
