where one reality, one transaction sequence in the shape of a ledger is maintained. A
distributed system is a hybrid form between central and (fully) decentral. It includes
a central unit (protocol), a number of hubs (full nodes) and many users (wallets).
With a distributed network, the nodes (users) on the one hand are connected sequentially, but there are several paths that lead to the final node, therefore several paths
can be followed simultaneously (parallel) between the first and final node.
Internet actually has the same structure. A ‘message reproduces itself’ via several
paths, from node to node, till the final note or the receiver has been reached. With
internet, messages are divided in small parcels that eventually merge again at the receiver. Therefore, the transport is secured better. You can compare this to using a
large money van to drive from A to Z. If this is robbed, you lose all your money.
You can also use two small cars to drive from A to Z via two different routes. The
notes have been cut through the middle so the serial numbers are no longer recognizable. At Z the notes are merged again to a unique number. If one car is robbed, it
has little use to the robbers because the money cannot be used without the second
part.
In principle, you can look at distributed systems in various ways. You can use a scale
for this and ask to what degree a certain perspective or aspect is organized central or
decentral:
Fully central
99 See paragraph 6.7 in book6.

Fully decentral

Boek8_Kopie_Intern_Gebruik_Weconomics

108

Using this scale you can ‘look’ at the degree of (de)centralization. You can view it
from the following perspectives:
▪ Physical, description of the physical architecture such as: which circuit of physical
processors or nodes are required in the network?
▪ Logical, description of logical architecture such as: what must be included in the
database, what is the physical position and what is the relationship between the
logical parts?
▪ Governance, who has the actual power such as: who decides what, with how many
votes?
▪ Logistics, how the total is structured such as: where is the Customer Order Decoupling Point, is there a general data utility facility to which applications can connect to and which application is used by the end user?
Uber and Airbnb for example are not connected to a general data utility facility. From
a logical point of view they are distributed at application level (buyers and sellers run
an application on their smartphone). In terms of governance they are managed centrally and the physical servers are probably installed decentralized. The way you could
organize a distributed network can be seen from different aspects:
▪ From the physical aspect, you look into a distributed network to see how computers or nodes are installed compared to each other. You also wonder: how many
computers must fail before the entire network fails? Can the network continue to
function when ten percent fails? Is there a critical path?
▪ From a logical aspect, you look at how data can be stored from a logical point of
view. The holistic character of your logical architecture plays a role in this: if you
would separate a part of the network, would the new separate parts still function
correctly? What is the smallest building block, what is a part and what is a total
and is there a logical hierarchy in this?
▪ From the governance aspect, you look at how many individuals or companies have
the actual power and how are decisions made.
▪ And finally, from the logistics aspect you will look at the structure of the network
and which layers there are: application, data contact, network and general data
utility facility.
In terms of governance, traditional companies are often organized centrally (there is
one CEO), and also centrally from a logical point of view (when you ‘cut off’ some
departments from the total, both parts can probably not immediately function independently). You could characterize a public blockchain like Bitcoin as decentral from
the physical and governance aspect and as central from a logical point of view (one
protocol and one shared reality). It seems that distributed systems only offer advantages, but there are also dangers:
▪ what if you cannot trust the designer or software developer?
▪ nodes use the same software, what if this contains an error?
▪ what if an update is suggested when everything is running correctly and the update
contains an error, or the creators have bad intentions?

Boek8_Kopie_Intern_Gebruik_Weconomics

109

▪ what if the aforementioned graphic maps to mine are produced by one company
and they can manipulate a blockchain via a backdoor?
▪ what if the number of miners reduces. You’ll get a form of centralization and these
miners also become nationalized or worse: they must be closed down.
A condition for peer-to-peer networks that use the Blockchain Organizing concept,
is that there are many sellers and many buyers (n:n community) and that the infrastructure construction and maintenance are separated from the usage. That people
can choose between different alternatives (applications, wallets, exchanges). With existing (social media) platforms these conditions are often not met. Initially internet
was also a distributed peer-to-peer platform and is now largely centralized. This
means that one should not only look at the infrastructure with Blockchain Organizing, but also at the power and independence aspect. In particular at the balance between scalability (centralization) and flexibility (decentralization). There must be a
good balance between cooperating and competing and between individual and company/government. With Blockchain Organizing we must not become dependent on
a few American tech companies with questionable intentions and non-transparent
algorithms, just as with internet. In fact, there must be a better balance between the
different stakeholders such as shareholders, maintainers, miners and users.
Smart contracts
A core attribute of capitalism is property. Capitalism needs a system with which we
can trust each other regarding property rights and the possible transfer thereof. Why
else would you invest your time in something that you are not sure of if it is, or will
be yours? To realize this we need a variety of lawyers, contracts, banks and notaries,
while this can also be done in a smarter way, for example, with a smart contract. A
smart contract is an algorithm that performs a number of actions (for example workflow), as soon as one or more conditions are met. For example, each time that you
log on to a website with the use of your Facebook or Google account, a smart contract is entered as it were. Behind the Facebook button that you use to log on there
is a set of agreements. You must first (and often only once) agree with these, before
you can continue to log on. Making an agreement about the use of your data, or for
example providing access to your address book, is often part of this set of agreements.
You can see a smart contract as the digitizing of a paper contract, but it is better to
see it as the digitizing of a workflow (collection of processes) between seller and
buyer. A smart contract is a mechanism to formalize and automate the relationship
between seller and buyer. The second characteristic is usually not possible without
the first characteristic. Before you can program processes or tasks you must first
formalize these, more or less linguistically (semantically) or mathematically, whereby
you agree that 1+1=2. So, with a smart contract, agreements are converted into programmable rules whereby the ‘If This Then That’ statement is one of the most commonly used rules.

Boek8_Kopie_Intern_Gebruik_Weconomics

110

Smart contracts reduce the transaction costs that are made with the preparation, execution, inspection and registration of transactions. Thereby users (often computers
with algorithms) can continuously learn from the result of a transaction (for example
to what extent does the outcome deviate from the expectation). By using evaluations,
you can make a better choice next time. The result of a measurement can also feed
another algorithm with a value (for example price or delivery time) so the values in a
smart contract can be adjusted what can then lead to another result with a next transaction.
The execution of a smart contract is less sensitive to human errors and manipulation.
What remains are errors, defects, incorrect estimates, manipulations, etcetera that can
be made by the designer and programmer of a smart contract. Smart contracts are
still sensitive to programming errors or attacks by hackers100. But when a smart contract is running smoothly and you cannot hack a contract against acceptable costs,
the risk of human errors at the execution will reduce. A computer does not easily
make a calculation error or make a mistake in a date and it has no problem performing a large amount of complex calculation in a short period of time. Plus, a faulttolerant distributed system will faster find a consensus on a value that is proposed
but not yet determined.
A smart contract also has less trouble with remembering the conditions that lead to
the performance of certain tasks or processes. The recording of the answer to a question whether the value of a condition has been reached or not is also much easier for
a smart contract to execute than it is for a person. A smart contract does not sleep,
is never tired or in a bad mood. This does not mean that we as users do not have to
do anything anymore. We must mainly think about the meaning and consequences
of technology for us, people. We have an important role in the design process and
the transition from used institutions to programmable institutions.
When we go back to the example where you use Facebook and Google to log on to
your favorite news site, you must trust that Facebook does its work correctly and
sends the right data to your news site. In this example, we still need a central trusted
third party, for the execution of a transaction. With Blockchain Organizing it is possible to skip the role of Facebook101. However, you must identify yourself once. This
is, as an example, possible via your bank or the city council where you live. Identification, authentication and the secure use of data will then be smarter with Blockchain
Organizing. With blockchain technology we try to address problems of fake work,
privacy, power of large tech companies and fake new. We can manage or solve these
problems if the various powers go into the right direction. The aforementioned consensus system will in particular play an important role in this. We will no longer depend on existing and often central institutions, to exchange data and to forward
100 Besides programming errors and hacks, smart contracts are also subject to other powers such

as legislation, human interaction, politics, economy and moderators.
101 However, we still need a trusted third party for linking the real and digital (virtual) world (also
called Oracle).

Boek8_Kopie_Intern_Gebruik_Weconomics

111

transactions, but we’ll have peer-to-peer systems whereby trust can be programmed
with mathematical proof as foundation. When we bring a smart contract on a blockchain, participants can find consensus on the value that is used for the execution of
a contract.
The final pieces of Nakamoto’s puzzle also seem important for the further development of smart contracts. An important part of smart contracts is letting go of your
own reality in relation to the reality of the counterparty, within the use of a shared
system. A system of distributed databases and a protocol to find consensus regarding
the shared reality within the system, will help with this. Therefore, two parties that
execute a transaction have the same reality about whether a condition has been met
and a transaction is or is not executed. They can operate within the same reality, they
view the same facts on which there is consensus that these are true. For instance:
about the price of a product or the agreed delivery time. With blockchain technology
it is possible to realize a shared reality about possessing a certain value or not, or
about including a transaction in a block or not. This creates a new shared reality as
basis for trust and for subsequent transactions.
Smart contracts are also very useful in situations with many parties (n:n communities), non-transparent markets, high transaction costs and few laws and regulations.
The blockchain that is created, after execution of the contract, becomes a shared
single source or reality. Information from a blockchain (ledger) can be used for a
number of processes such as suggested in figure 3.8 (smart contract between supply
and demand). Examples of sub-processes with the execution of a smart contract are:
profiling, selecting, approving, ordering, delivering, paying and registering.
We finish this part with naming a number of advantages of smart contracts with
respects to the current institutions:
▪ Costs: lower (transaction costs) because fewer intermediaries and institutions are
required.
▪ Objectivity: less human interpretation possible.
▪ Productivity: increases because people can do the same work in less time. For
example, they have to retype or interpret less data. This also reduces the time for
fixing errors.
▪ Speed: executing different processes and finding consensus is possible with the
speed of internet and the speed of the connected processors.
▪ Risk: less risk because there is less interpretation and human interaction and therefore the risk of failure of parts is reduced.
▪ Reliability: a computer makes fewer calculation errors than man, forgets less and
can perform routine processes much better and quicker in a relatively quiet environment.
▪ Security: less risk of human errors when transferring messages.
▪ Meaning: by using a joint ontology, taxonomy and synonyms list, the human language will become susceptible to fewer interpretations, will improve communication and thus reduce the chance of conflicts.

Boek8_Kopie_Intern_Gebruik_Weconomics

112

▪ Maintenance: as soon as there is for example 90% consensus on a new release of
the protocol, everyone will then use the same protocol at the same (logical) time.
There are many applications for smart contracts. One example is an application in
the shared economy where shared bikes, shared cars and shared rooms contain a chip
that can be activated with your smartphone. As soon as you have accepted the conditions and complied with these, including the payment, you can open the shared
room with your smartphone. There are also applications for offline shopping: you
can just take clothes and other goods from the store. Only if you have an insufficient
account balance you will not be able to pass the detection gates.
Decentralized Autonomous Organizations (DAO)
There are no (human) managers in a DAO, just as there are not drivers anymore in
a self-driving car. A DAO protocol enables participants (these no longer have to be
humans) to make transactions with each other without central authority such as management. Managers will not disappear, but fewer will be required and their roles will
change. The core of management includes a mechanical part (planning, distributing
resources, checking, registering) and an organizational part (motivating, developing).
These are all actions to reduce insecurity whereby especially the mechanical part can
be automated.
As we have often seen in this book, we want to reduce the insecurity between demand (finalization) and supply (specialists), but this can be done in various ways.
Many coordinating actions that are done by managers involve bringing supply and
demand together (connecting people and resources to a task, assignment, project or
problem). The way that managers do this (this in the meaning of: ‘... if this is the
situation I must do that...’), can be converted into a policy or recipe. So, it can be
converted into a kind of recipe with which the organization can control itself. Mechanical management is then replaced by a protocol that enables participants to find
consensus themselves. Participants in the decision making all have the same shared
reality and can all make a proposal that others vote on. In some situations, for example the processing of transactions, DAOs are much more able to share information
and to make decisions, than organizations that use a social script. A social script can
be motivated by culture, interpretation or emotions for example and act differently
in decisions whether a transaction took place or not.
A DAO has no traditional company boundaries, only smallest building blocks, connections, instructions and conditions, contained in a protocol. Of course, you can
incorporate as a limitation that an actor of a DAO may not communicate outside the
boundary of the DAO whereby you determine the boundary in advance by making
connections. But this limitation is a simple ‘yes’ or ‘no’ in a protocol. A connection
that you can switch on and off.
The separation of information provision between companies is much more complex
and much more difficult to adjust with the current working method, than within and

Boek8_Kopie_Intern_Gebruik_Weconomics

113

between DAOs. With traditional companies, you have to deal with IT systems, collective employment agreements, buildings and fixed contracts. With DAOs you
mainly have to deal with building blocks, connections, instructions, data and software
code. Legislation may change but the principles with which you can execute a smart
contract will not change often because they can be reduced to an IFTTT statement.
A DAO itself often does not have an impact on legalization or government rules, for
example: a postal company has to replace flex-work contracts for permanent contracts, but a DAO can easily change the value of a contract or connection.
For a DAO any contract is just a set of variables that can change. The values of
variables can change but the principles will stay. The relationship between seller of
work and buyer of work is a connection for a DAO. There are different types of
connections for this whether or not made possible by politics. But the data and software to perform a certain type of connections, does not have to be influenced by
politics, provided you have correctly addressed this from the design. This also applies
to the performance of governmental transaction such as benefits, grants, allowances
and taxes. DAOs are also little affected by mergers and takeovers. What is created
after a merger is a new collection of connections that in the core (ontology) will not
change through a merger. This offers cost benefits for IT. In addition to this advantage, within DAOs ideas will emerge and be used more effectively and efficiently.
Each actor can propose an idea or software change for instance and the network
votes on whether to use recourses for this or not. Working with other organizational
forms such as the DAO, also requires another expertise of organizational advisors.
You can compare this with the car industry: in the past, the mechanics mainly had a
background in mechanical engineering, but now more and more in electronics and
computer science.

Ethereum
An initiative that works on the practical implementation of DAOs is Ethereum. In
2013 Ethereum was started by a number of programmers, led by Vitalik Buterin who
is also involved in the Bitcoin community. The project started with the publication
of a white paper by Buterin called “A Next-Generation Smart contract and Decentralized
Application Platform.” The financing of the project was made possible by raising eighteen million dollars with crowd funding. Ethereum started as an internet project that
wanted to realize the recording and exchange of information, decentral and without
the intervention of traditional institutions. It is an extension of the Bitcoin protocol
that mainly focuses on digital money. Ethereum wants to be able to facilitate more
different values than just money. Gradually the project has grown into a platform
with which you can develop applications that work with blockchain technology. You
can also put applications on a blockchain yourself. The underlying mathematical
principles of blockchain technology are not always immediately clear to many developers, but that is not necessary.

Boek8_Kopie_Intern_Gebruik_Weconomics

114

Ethereum ensures that the development of applications based on blockchain technology, becomes accessible to a larger group of developers. Ethereum could develop
into some kind of Appstore for blockchain applications. The different decentral applications, also called DAPPs102 can for example be used for social media services
(such as Steemit), cloud storage services (such as Dropbox) and even a replacement
for supercomputers (such as Watson). An important motive for Buterin is the development of technology that will make the economy and community more inclusive,
safe, free, efficient and reliable. According to Buterin, a decentralized economy and
community is a logical consequence of the current system and manifests itself in
movements such as the shared economy, the interactive Web and more recently:
blockchain. Ethereum ensures that it becomes easier to development applications
with the use of blockchain technology and on a blockchain. Bitcoin focuses more on
digital money, Ethereum focuses more on messages via social media, storage services, contracts and other peer-to-peer transactions and the recording of values.
Currently Ethereum is mainly an open source, public platform based on blockchain
technology for the development of smart contracts. The software code itself is put
on a blockchain as value so you cannot mutate this. Once the code is in the blockchain, you cannot change this anymore. However, you can create a new value and
put this in the blockchain as transaction that replaces old software, but you cannot
undo transactions that have been made.
This concludes the description of the different technical concepts as part of blockchain technology and therefore Blockchain Organizing. We will continue with the
application thereof within new organizations (models).
3.5 Blockchain for communities
We as authors are mainly interested in blockchains for communities. These are hybrid forms between fully central and decentral. As there are many types of communities this requires some explanation. A community is often seen as a company that
forms a community with its customers, or a council with its citizens. We call this 1:n
community. One organization maintains a connection with many of its members,
citizens, customers or employees. Often communities are associated with an online
community or group on LinkedIn or Facebook. Most of these communities were
created at the beginning of the twenty-first century. They were created from a technology and business push. What we mean by communities are n:n communities:
many sellers and many buyers who cooperate with smart contracts in a shared transaction network. Most existing n:n communities that are currently engaged in sharing
information and executing transactions, are platforms or business communities 103
like Uber and Airbnb. There is hardly any decentralized authority or financing possibilities with this.
102 DAPP: Decentralized Application.

103 Examples are Amazon ore Bol.com, companies that organizes payments and logistics when

you offer products via their platform.

Boek8_Kopie_Intern_Gebruik_Weconomics

115

Given the experiences with Facebook104 and the Uber-drivers protests for example,
the next generation of n:n communities will no longer belong to one company, but
will be more decentralized, not just in terms of infrastructure but also in terms of
governance. They not only enable information sharing but also making transactions:
not depending on time, place and organization. Plus, more and more communities
will be connected to a shared transaction network, which again is connected to a
general data utility facility. To communicate and make transactions with each other,
you use community software, similar to wallet software for Bitcoin. Community software will more and more replace company software. The thinking unit with organizing and automating demand and supply will change more and more from company
to collaborations and eventually shared transaction networks. Transferring (valuable)
information is also a transaction. The insecurity in transactions will increasingly be
reduced by protocols that will replace existing institutions such as purchase and sales
conditions. We are at the beginning of a network economy whereby companies will
review their own role in solving management problems and organizing trust. Because
companies and non-transparent markets have high transaction costs, they will be increasingly replaced by cheaper resources. Companies will probably become more and
more a major node (hub) in shared transaction networks.
The firm and the market as ideal forms
Cooperating and partnerships play an important role within Blockchain Organizing.
For example, when we look at the organization of work, we currently choose between doing the work ourselves, via the own company, or outsourcing the work via
the market. A partnership is usually seen as an organizational form between these
two extremes: company and market105. Important differences between these two default forms are:
▪ The firm: a company performs processes itself. For example, Philips a Dutch
Electronics concern, at the beginning of the twentieth century. They produced the
glass for the light bulb and the paper for packaging themselves because the transaction costs would be too high when outsourced. This form of organizing is used
when the need for cooperation is clear and long term. The extent to which joint
decisions are taken and tasks, authorities and responsibilities are structured, is
higher than for the market. A company is mainly scalable in its structure, but not
so flexible.
▪ The market: when temporary employment agency provides a temporary worker
to a company, it is a market transaction as the temporary worker does not gain
full employment. This form of cooperation is short term (only the transaction)
and the degree to which organization make agreements together is low (at the
most a purchase contract or provision agreement). This form of organizing normally involves high transaction costs because you must search for a supplier, enter
104 The unauthorized use of Facebook data via Cambridge Analytica, for example.

105 See also Ben Gomes-Casseres in the Strategic Organization magazine (Vol 1(3):327-33): ‘Com-

petitive advantage in alliance constellations’ (Augustus 2003).

Boek8_Kopie_Intern_Gebruik_Weconomics

116

separate contracts and you require institutions to reduce the insecurity. A market
is mainly flexible, but not so scalable.
In principle, cooperating is inherent to organizing. Cooperating mainly involves the
coordinating function between supply and demand. There is cooperation, but also
competition, both within companies as well as between buyers and sellers in a market. Companies and markets are examples of partnerships. Cooperating constitutes
a main part of organizing and can be seen as a counterpart to competing. When you
distribute tasks to specialists (competitors), coordination or cooperation is necessary
in order to achieve the desired end result. In recent years the western world has been
shaped mainly by Darwin’s evolution theory who assumed that animals must compete with each other to survive. But Darwin conducted a research on the Galapagos
Islands where there was overpopulation. The Russian anarchist Peter Kropotkin conducted a research in Siberia at about the same time, where there was under-population. Here animals had to cooperate to survive.
So both can be helpful to survive: competition and cooperation. It depends on the
situation and time which is best in any case. Both in philosophy and organizational
theory the concept of ‘contingency’ plays an import role. In philosophy is means that
every objectively perceivable object or subject has something coincidental that could
be true but also be false, that could just as well have been different. It has properties
that accidentally happen to it. In organizational theory it means that can be stated
that characteristics of an object of research depend on various factors from the environment of the object. The contingency approach is based on the idea that every
environment requires adapted behavior. See for example Lawrence and Lorsch, who
developed an open systems theory of how organizations and organizational sub-units
adapt to best meet the demands of their immediate environment.
The examples from nature (Darwin and Kropotkin) are also applicable to our socialeconomical order. Due to aging in European societies we are increasingly dealing
with ‘under-population’, so to increase productivity it is better to cooperate than to
compete. The main issue is that both (competing and cooperating), depending on
context and finalization, must be able to take place in good balance. Blockchain Organizing with a suitable infrastructure and management model, plays an important
role in this. To find a good balance between cooperating and competing there are
five important organizational principles of Blockchain Organizing: ‘specialize, coordinate & finalize’; ‘programmable institutions’; ‘structured flexibility’; ‘apart together’
and ‘back to basic’. These are all addressed in paragraph 2.5.
A partnership or community can be positioned on the scale between the two extremes: the company and the market. Cooperating is often linked to the company
and competing to the market. But we all know that there is also competition within
companies. We also know that competitors sometimes cooperate in the market place.
The idea that a company hardly has any transaction costs is also not correct. When
we no longer take the company as unit of thinking, but the smallest building block,

Boek8_Kopie_Intern_Gebruik_Weconomics

117

the human being, a company also has transaction costs. People have to be recruited
for a project, agreements must be made and there must be a safety net in case something goes wrong. In addition, companies have a turnover of staff what leads to
higher transaction costs. People compete and cooperate, within and between organizations.
A community that works with the principles of Blockchain Organizing can both be
scalable as well as flexible. Cooperating and competing are in balance and the transaction costs are minimal. A Blockchain organization is preferably a partnership, but
not all partnerships are blockchain organized. For example, a joint venture (partnership) can also become a company that performs transactions via the market and cooperates internally based on its own business culture. Therefore, it is not necessarily
a partnership that is blockchain organized. A partnership is often implemented in a
new fiscal legal entity. The NAM (a Dutch oil & gas company), for example, is a joint
venture of Shell (50%) and Exxon Mobil (50%). The NAM itself is also organized as
a ‘normal company’.
Cooperation options
To be able to properly place Blockchain Organizing it is useful to make a distinction
between the different cooperation options that can apply to organizations (we do not
mean the outsourcing of work here). Roughly we see a number of cooperation options between organizations:

1. Bilateral contracts and agreements
This cooperation mainly focuses on a 1:1 relationship, between two existing fiscal
legal entities. Usually an agreement is concluded between two companies (but it can
also be countries or councils for example). Examples are:
▪ Joint venture: usually a new entity formed by two organizations with two or more
shareholders; has its own management and systems.
▪ Alliance, cooperation, franchise or distribution agreement.
2. Classic community
A classic partnership has a 1:n relationship. One (1) organization enters a relationship
with its customers, citizens or for example its members (n). Microsoft for instance
has an online community with its users. The communities are often created by the
rise of Web2.0: the interactive web. With the use of these communities it is easy to
share target group-oriented information and perform transactions. Companies can
make transactions online and communicate with their customer or for example councils with their citizens. But classic partnerships essentially change little in the way
production and consumption is organized. The producer, the government authority
or the company still dictates the way consumers, citizens and employees must communicate and transact. Only the modality or medium, from offline to online, from
physical to digital, changes.

Boek8_Kopie_Intern_Gebruik_Weconomics

118

3. Modern community
A modern community is characterized by a n:n relationship: many sellers and buyers
cooperate smartly in a shared transaction network. The network is often provided by
an independent network provider that executes and settles the transactions as a fiscallegal entity under supervision of a foundation. This network provider is part of a trias
politica system with a foundation that represents the interests of users and a supervisor. One can also opt for a (new) fiscal-legal entity such as a cooperative society
(co-op). In any case a governance structure is chosen that usually also includes a
supervisor and where the various stakeholders make policy and choices in a democratic way. In this form cooperation mainly means sharing information, communicating and making transactions, from the smallest building block via a shared network or shared service center above and beyond the boundaries of existing companies, whereby no party, or parties, can play a dominant role. In addition to the first
two forms of cooperation, the modern community offers the possibility to transfer
certain processes of the company to a new building and shared transaction network.
Cooperating with blockchain principles
As company, you usually have a vision: what is happening to the world, with my
company, my market? As a company, you choose a certain mission (where do I
stand?). Finally, you choose a target and target group and you develop a strategy.
Strategy can be seen as the way you want to reach your goals. You usually require
people and resources for the implementation of the strategy. With Blockchain Organizing, mainly the cohesion, the connection between the smallest elements is important. Because you very rarely will have infinite resources and capabilities, it is necessary to cooperate with your environment. You can so this by using your own systems and rules (we usually call this outsourcing), but you can also realize a cooperation within a joint venture or alliance (1:1) or classic community (1:n) or for example
modern community (n:n). When you lack certain capabilities as an organization, you
have a number of options, for example you can:
▪ train people or hire expertise
▪ outsource work
▪ take over a (part of a) company
▪ enter a cooperation
All these options have the purpose to add value, make connections and perform
transactions. A certain choice usually has advantages and disadvantages. In economic
theory, these can usually be traced back to the transaction costs and you could also
call these ‘connection costs’. It is generally assumed that the connection costs within
a company are lower than the transaction costs between companies (via a market).
In the past, the connection costs (transport, communication, coordination and risk)
were relatively high with physical production and companies did most of the work
themselves. But, with the switch from physical to digital production and consumption, the arrival of internet and now the rise of Blockchain Organizing, the

Boek8_Kopie_Intern_Gebruik_Weconomics

119

connection costs in a digital community are almost zero. Oliver Williamson106 is seen
as one of the founders of an economic theory that can be used for Blockchain Organizing.
According to Williamson, organizations exist because of the limited span or control
of a human being (insecurity reduction in managing people) and because of the high
connection costs associated with outsourcing or cooperating. Managers can only
manage a limited number of people. Companies use a structure with a hierarchy to
solve this, whereby a team is not greater than, for example, fifty people and a departmental staff does not exceed one hundred and fifty people. In doing so, a certain
business culture is developed that causes employees, more or less automatically, to
adhere to the desired behavior. It is thought that scalability is an advantage of a company. Due to structure and culture you can use a larger span or control and you
probably have less connection costs. A disadvantage of a company, with high fixed
costs, is in any case that it is less flexible. Basically, Williamson’s theory means that
you could choose between concluding separate contracts (connections) to realize a
certain objective. Separate connections with individual people and resources. The
alternative is to conclude one contract with one company, some kind of black box,
or nexus of contracts that has already organized the separate connections in its structure and culture. Both alternatives have advantages and disadvantages. You can see
them as the two extremes of a scale.
You can work with:
1. separate connections or transactions via the market;
2. a collection of fixed connections via a company.
An example of a combination of these two applications can be found in the movie
industry. It would not be smart to employ actors because the demand is different
every time. Actors are casted and usually work as freelancers. However, the equipment is used in every film. So, you can permanently employ lighting, sound and camera people and therefore save transaction costs to select and contract them every
time. There are all kinds of partnerships between the two extremes of doing it yourself and outsourcing. The Blockchain Organizing concept is fairly new because it
primarily focuses on a new balance between demand and supply, structure and flexibility, cooperating and competing and between separate and together. Blockchain
Organizing has low transactions costs because the connection, the coordinating capacity between demand and supply is programmed in a protocol. This makes Blockchain Organizing scalable and flexible.
Classic organizing takes existing companies and markets (metaphor: Lego building
kit), as principle. Blockchain Organizing takes organizing capacity (metaphor: Lego
building blocks) as principle. Classic organizing often (implicitly) reasons from the
106 See for example also paragraph 2.6 in this book and the Weconomics analysis book, paragraph

5.9: ‘Making choices and considering interests’.

Boek8_Kopie_Intern_Gebruik_Weconomics

120

supply (what is already there). Blockchain Organizing (explicitly) reasons from the
demand and starts to organize (connect) people and resources within a certain organizational model, transition program and certain infrastructure, at minimum connection costs. If you, as an organization, want to outsource a certain process, you
can do this by entering cooperation with a community. For example: a group of selfemployed people, united in a co-op107. With Blockchain Organizing you can set up
the process of searching and booking flex workers in a community. Many hirers (n)
and many portfolio workers (n) cooperate in this n:n community. The community
has the advantage that the relationship between scalability and flexibility is often better than within a company or via a market. Blockchain Organizing does not only
provide a better balance between scalability and flexibility, but also between cooperating and competing, part and total.
Examples of blockchain communities
The main experience that we have and use as writers of this book, is our involvement
(since the rise of internet), in the setup of n:n communities. We are convinced that
Blockchain Organizing works especially well in n:n communities. In figure 3.7 you
see an example of a n:n community for the Dutch training market. In this example,
you see three sellers and buyers, but the principle also works with 300, 3000 or 3
million sellers and buyers.

Figure 3.7: Example blockchain community for the Dutch training market

Between supply and demand you could develop and implement a smart contract
(programmed workflow where for example a copy can put in a public library when
the document is approved or where step 3 can start when 2 is finished). In figure:
107 In December of 2012 a collective of sixty self-employed lawyers wins a European tender for

the provision of legal services to the Ministry of Economic affairs in the Netherlands. These sixty
lawyers are supported by the central organization: ‘Network of Enterprising Lawyers’. They
smartly cooperate via a shared (information) network.

Boek8_Kopie_Intern_Gebruik_Weconomics

121

Figure 3.8: Smartcontract between supply and demand

Since the rise of internet, we have built up experience with organizing process step 1
through 3. Step 1 is creating a profile of the product/service (the value, for example
a time management course) that you offer. Step 2 is searching and selecting a value
and step 3 the purchase and delivery of the value. Step 4, the payment or the delivery
of the counter-value will now be designed by using blockchain technology. Examples
of n:n communities whereby we have used or use these principles of Blockchain
Organizing are: Buro Staff Support, for the combination deployment of trainees and
advisors in consultancy projects, Confluent Logistics to better organize supply
chains, Prodis for the Dutch training market, School for Cooperating for a fundamentally new perspective on cooperating between positions and organization and
Weconet Blockchain Technologies, a community for Blockchain Organizing. In addition, we currently cooperate in the setup of a HR, care and social coins community,
and in the PLATO project for a smarter cooperation between different company
positions to address the challenges of the new privacy legislation GDPR, which is
further explained in paragraph 5.3.
3.6 Blockchain standardization
Many of the agreements that we make with each other, especially in political terms,
are agreements between people and therefore also interpretable by people. According
to George Packer108, politics (also politics within companies) is about transactions, it
is opaque and consists of hierarchical power relationships. They are sealed with handshakes and not recorded. When they are recorded, it is ensured that messages can be
adjusted or interpreted afterwards. Technology however is empirical and often transparent, driven by data. This means that we can reduce insecurities in transactions
108 Journalist at The New Yorker.

Boek8_Kopie_Intern_Gebruik_Weconomics

122

with the use of protocols, by removing the subjective interpretation of man. Whether
‘politics’ or ‘man’ also want this, is a different matter altogether. We can best explain
the difference between politics/man and technology, and between the ‘social script’
and the ‘technical script’, with the Pythagorean Theorem:
In Politics: A2+B2 could be equal to C2
In Technology: A2+B2=C2
A protocol is a behavior or implementation agreement, usually in the form of a number of steps to be implemented sequentially, in parallel and / or 'under condition'.
An example is the Internet Protocol (IP): an agreement whereby computers can communicate with each other in a network. When a protocol (by the international community) is structured, formalized and recognized, we usually call this a (ISO) standard. A standard usually is a document in which agreements are structural recorded
and as such recognized, acknowledged and controllable. They could be related to a
product, service, process or method. A protocol can lead to an ISO standard. So, you
could see a standard as a protocol recognized by the international community.
As indicated before, you can regard blockchain as an additional layer to internet while
internet can be seen as a network to share information peer-to-peer. Blockchain can
be looked at as a network to execute transactions peer-to-peer without having to
trust each other. Therefore, we also call blockchain the Trust Web. Internet can only
exist because we have made agreements with each other about how we prepare (provide), send (transport) and receive (request) data with a (computer) device. Because
we must be able to trust each other that this process is going well, we reduce the
insecurity by developing a protocol and by agreeing, standardizing, recognizing and
normalizing its use. So, to make reliable transactions we use agreements and programmable trust. Examples of these agreements or (uniform) protocols for internet
are109:
▪ TCP (Transmission Control Protocol)
▪ IP (Internet Protocol)
▪ URL (Uniform Resource Locator)
▪ HTML (HyperText Markup Language)
▪ XML (Extensible Markup Language)
▪ HTTP (HyperText Transfer Protocol)
Currently there is no (uniform) protocol for blockchain. However, there is a Bitcoin
protocol that ‘automatically’ performs a number of processes at the transport level
of Bitcoin from seller to buyer. You can also see Bitcoin as proof that blockchain
technology works. But this does not mean that the Bitcoin protocol will automatically
become the (yet to be developed, or agreed on) blockchain protocol Parts of the
Bitcoin protocol are for example the:
109 For more explanation, see also the Weconomics practice book, paragraph 3.4: ‘Weconomics

and the World Wide Web’.

Boek8_Kopie_Intern_Gebruik_Weconomics

123

▪ downloading of wallet or mining software
▪ collecting and subsequently finding ‘close’ neighbors
▪ creating private and public keys and digital signatures
▪ implementing hash functions
▪ implementing mining
▪ managing and removing unsorted transactions
▪ including a block in the blockchain
So, the Bitcoin protocol is not the same as the blockchain protocol. As stated, there
is no standard for a blockchain protocol yet. If you are now investing, or betting on
the wrong 'standard', you could become the Video 2000 (Philips and Grundig) or
Betamax (Sony) system of the video recording technology. Eventually, VHS (JVC)
became the standard. Currently there are developments that can contribute to the
creation of a blockchain protocol and standard. There are standards under development that indicate how we can determine, manage and share a certain identity quality
in a similar way. There are also standards (being developed) to create a personal profile, to enter a training course or vacancy or to execute an economic transaction.
To be able to make a shared transaction network, in which existing organizations
cooperate and function properly, it is important to make agreements about the exchange of data. This is often a sticky and lengthy process because existing organizations have their own interests and (legacy) systems. A newly established network organization or community will have fewer problems with this. Based on the last insights and technology, a new blockchain organization or DAO can be set up and
subsequently the existing internal processes can be transferred to the new construction. However, this new construction will eventually have to deal with standards,
which it probably contributed to, but often cannot determine. The advantage of
blockchain technology is that it already contains a certain standardization: it is open
source and set up by a community. This is different from a situation where software
is developed within companies involving patent applications and a push to become
‘the standard’. Nicolas Cary, cofounder and CEO of the company Blockchain says
the following about this110: “It’s hard to regulate the blockchain technology in this sense: It’s
not centralized, there’s no overall committees or the general authority. However, it’s already regulated
by its open-source nature and the fact that it’s regulated by mathematics.”
Webservices and APIs are currently also widely used to exchange data, but this is
often laborious and increases the risk of data leaks or unauthorized use of personal
data. So, we are looking for more sustainable solutions to securely, meaningfully and
reliably transport data from seller to buyer, to store it and to wrap it. Eventually we
will be able to use a general data utility facility, similar to the electricity network.

110 See article by Alicia Naumoff in the Cointelegraph: ‘Blockchain Co-Founder Nicolas Cary:

You Can Be Your Own Bank’ (11-4-2016).

Boek8_Kopie_Intern_Gebruik_Weconomics

124

In paragraph 6.3 of book6 we give a few examples111 of initiatives in terms of standardization that can be useful for making sure shared transactions networks function
properly. For example, it is important to make agreements about a Personal Data
Service, when companies no longer have their own data to perform processes. Personal Data Service on a blockchain is an important application of Blockchain Organizing. Agreements in terms of identity management and authentication are important
parts of Blockchain Organizing where agreements are necessary. The mentioned initiatives indicate the important and possible effect of standards and protocols. Later
on, they can contribute to the development of a blockchain protocol and the standardization and normalization thereof. Most initiatives are private-public cooperation,
illustrating that cooperating and open-innovation is important for Blockchain Organizing.
Open standards
In the Weconomics practice book112 we already extensively discussed the development and the use of open data and standards. New for example is a recently adopted
motion113 that makes it mandatory to use open standards by the Dutch government.
This obligation is included in the GDI. The Generic Digital Infrastructure Act will
then fall under administrative law. With the new law the House of Representatives
can forcefully ensure that the government applies as many open standards as possible.
The House, having heard the deliberations, considering that the Open Government Action Plan
2016-2017 will focus on actively making public sector information available and stimulating an
open attitude and behavior among governments; considering that the use of open standards is
essential in actively making information available to citizens and in addition ensures for more
freedom of choice in ICT suppliers, promoting legal certainty, reducing administrative burdens
and being able to work efficiently and in chains as a single government; considering that open
source software provides more competition in the software market, so the government can
stipulate better conditions and prices for software; noting that open standards and open source
software are not yet used everywhere within the national government and local authorities and
that is partly due to the lack of knowledge and expertise with the contracting of software; requests the government to oblige the use of open standards by law; also requests the government
to make information it has on open source software available via a knowledge center, and proceeds to the order of the day.
(Motion of House of Representative the Netherlands: Oosenburg, proposed 4 October 2016 )

According to MP Astrid Oosenburg, the new legislation will soon lead to the choice
of open source or free software in a tender, in case of equal suitability. If, for example, this is not yet technically possible, an exit strategy must be drawn up detailing
how a government organization can terminate a contract with a non-open source
supplier within a maximum of five years.
111 No full overview, only to illustrate. We based our opinion regarding these examples on public

information.
112 See also the Weconomics practice book, paragraph 5.3.5: ‘Free and open source’.
113 Motion of the Dutch Member of Parliament: Astrid Oosenburg (4-10-2016).

Boek8_Kopie_Intern_Gebruik_Weconomics

125

Top-down versus bottom-up
Most standards that are developed are based on the existing world. Many IT projects
focus on the automation of analogue processes, without questioning these analogous
processes or the institutions that perform them. They try to simplify the existing
reality, with all its complexity, by standardizing it. This is a long term, time-consuming and expensive process that provides a lot of employment, but with little effect. It
is better to start with a new organizational design that already contains a lot of simplicity. A design that reasons from agreements, building blocks and principles and
reduces insecurity, without increasing the complexity. The only fruitful way to come
to a shared and long-term standard is: working from a joint ontology.
A number of fundamental questions, on top of the used technical, legal and economic aspects, regarding the initiatives for standardization and regulations explained
in this paragraph, are:
▪ To what extent is the organizational aspect taken into account?
▪ Are there thoughts about a new organizational model or design?
▪ What is the role of the subjective person in the performance of processes?
▪ Do these initiatives take the existing ideal form such as the firm, the government
and non-transparent markets as their starting point and unit of analysis or not?
It almost seems impossible to take into account a large number of existing standards
and hundreds, maybe thousands of companies with their own interests and their own
IT systems. With many initiatives, it is unclear whether Greenfield organizing has
been chosen. With Greenfield organizing you reason from the agreements (that
transactions and standards have in it), and set up a new network and transfer existing
processes from companies to networks. If we look at the way the internet standardization has taken place we must conclude that existing organizations played a big role
in this114. This is probably one of the reasons why internet has fundamentally not
changed a lot in the way we organize demand and supply. Pilot projects of the Dutch
government seem to indicate that existing institutions and analogue processes are
taken as unit of thinking and analysis in the design of blockchain processes.
New Organizing means that you must confront the status quo and your stakeholders,
where it is not self-evident that firms, government institutions or banks continue to
carry out transactions. It would be smart not to develop a blockchain standard on
basis of the existing organizational model, but to immediately eliminate the boundaries of companies and to think and work from the smallest building blocks, connections and a shared transaction network. When existing organizations resist this, it can
mean that you, in setting up blockchain projects, miss the connection of mainly large
organizations in the beginning and mainly cooperate with start-ups, the SME and
employee co-ops.

114 See also W3 consortium in Weconomics analysis book, paragraph 6.6: ‘Internet of the people’.

Boek8_Kopie_Intern_Gebruik_Weconomics

126

Bitcoin protocol
Nobody, but everybody is ‘owner’ of the Bitcoin network and protocol. You can
compare this with the fact that nobody is owner of the protocol that makes e-mail
traffic possible. The Bitcoin protocol is controlled, updated and managed by a community of Bitcoin users and developers. Software developers are continuously improving the software but cannot enforce changes in the Bitcoin protocol without
convincing others of the use, necessity and efficiency. Users are free to choose which
software (version) they use. To stay compatible, nodes must use software that can
cooperate with a lower level (stack). The Bitcoin protocol only works when a certain
degree of agreement has been reached between the users. This can be compared with
the required agreement in the Linux community115. To make the Bitcoin Protocol
and the underlying principles work for, say, decentralization work, users and developers know that they must find consensus. But this principle is not much different
from finding consensus on traffic rules for example. The difference is that we use a
community in case of Bitcoin, while traffic rules are decided by representatives of
the people. Although the latter is not completely correct, because city councils for
example may also use certain regulations that affect traffic.

Blockchain protocol and standardization
In his book ‘The Fourth Industrial Revolution’ Klaus Schwab116 writes: “The digital
revolution is creating radically new approaches that revolutionize the way in which individuals and
institutions engage and collaborate. For example, the blockchain, often described as a distributed
ledger, is a secure protocol where a network of computers collectively verifies a transaction before it
can be recorded and approved117.” In Schwab’s vision, blockchain essentially is a shared,
programmable, cryptographic, secure and reliable ledger, without central control,
that can be checked by nodes in the network on input reference and double spending.
Different blockchain protocols will be created. After the Bitcoin protocol, mainly the
development of the Ethereum platform is currently important. You could see this as
a blockchain of software program code en decentral applications. Currently we see,
mainly in the financial sector, a multitude of different blockchains and blockchain
companies. Different technologies and protocols are used for different applications,
whereby there is little agreement yet on a standard to be used such as TCP/IP with
internet. Large companies preferably shield their innovations and ensure that customers are faced with a vendor lock-in. There is little consideration for interoperability, because this is not in the interest of an existing company. Even a number of
important blockchain principles are abandoned. An example is the ability to adjust a
transaction after this has been agreed between the different nodes. New technology
115 There is not just one Linux community, but several. However, there is one Linux Foundation

to promote, protect and improve ‘collaborative development’ of Linux. Linux is an open-source
control system.
116 Klaus Swab (1938- ), founder and chairman of the World Economic Forum.
117 See paragraph 2.1: ‘Megatrends’ in that book.

Boek8_Kopie_Intern_Gebruik_Weconomics

127

competes with existing organizational forms and interests. Therefore, technical possibilities lose their strength and the risk occurs that companies with the most financial
means, pull the standard towards them and shape this in their own interest. Because
these are usually large, wealthy companies, there is a high risk that blockchain, just as
internet, will change little in our productivity, privacy and powerlessness of people
and small entrepreneurs.
But there is also a growing awareness and also maybe belief that we (and we mainly
mean us people, small entrepreneurs and in a wider context the European Union or
maybe the United Nations), are able to do things differently this time. We know the
interoperability of data and standardization is necessary to use the complete potential
and capacity of blockchain technology. But in the bottom layer data consists of zeros
and ones, that is also a form of standardization. The obstacle is not in the standardization but the fact that almost every company wants to have its own database. Even
when we look at the new European privacy law there is a need for data transport
from one system to the other, if the user requests this. It is better not to connect a
blockchain to a company or government but directly to the smallest building block
from the start. Starting points for blockchain standardization should be the individual
who, for example, has his or her own portfolio, patient file, consumer file and citizen
file. This principle is far away from the interests of the most used (social media)
platforms and business models of large companies. We know that Facebook is designed so you are tempted as much as possible to click on adverts and to spend as
much time as possible on it. It is not designed to easily take your own data, to make
transactions with parties that do not pay Facebook.
Besides the development of diverging and eventually possibly converging blockchain
protocols, the need for blockchain standards will also increase. There are already
some private, public and private/public initiatives, but there is still a lot of work to
be done. Apart from the technical work, we believe that it is especially important to
work on awareness that blockchain will change little if we do not change the model
of organizing demand and supply. To illustrate this better, we list a number of initiatives in terms of cooperation and standardization below:
▪ The Hyperledger Project118 of the Linux Foundation: is a common effort to develop blockchain technology by developing domain crossing open standards for
distributed ledger. Many large organizations are a member.
▪ Ripple Lab119: introduces the InterLedger Protocol (ILP), a free open source protocol for secure and efficient payments in a network.
▪ Digital Asset Holding: uses Distributed Ledger Technology for ‘tracing and tracking’ financial assets.
▪ R3 CEV consortium: worldwide consortium of large banks managed by the company R3. With 42 banks a test period of sixty days is used to use five different
blockchains to spend, trade and redeem financial products.
118 See also

website: hyperledger.org

119 See also website: ripple.com/technology

Boek8_Kopie_Intern_Gebruik_Weconomics

128

▪ B3i consortium: consortium of insurers who start a payment platform based on
blockchain technology. The project initially focuses on the reduction of paper
work and the cheaper setup and execution of insurance contacts. B3i is a first step
to a standard for blockchain within the insurance world.
▪ Muskoka Group: On the initiative of Don and Alex Tapscott120, a group of blockchain experts meets in 2016. The group primarily does not focus (yet) on standardization, but actually on the stage before awareness. To properly use the potential of blockchain we must first agree on a number of principles, before we are
able to standardize.
▪ The International Organization for Standardization (ISO): To be able to improve
data exchange between users and applications, the standardization institute of
Australia suggests to develop international standards for blockchain technology
ISO has asked all 161 members to submit the proposal to the national stakeholders.
So in 2016 ISO also started a consultation round in response to the proposal from
Australia to investigate the desirability for standards in terms of blockchain technology. Opinions differ on the extent to which and the speed with which we can come
to standardization. People rightly fear a similar development as with the standardization of internet, whereby via the World Wide Web Consortium (W3C) mainly large
(American) tech companies had a lot of influence121. The central influence is at odds
with the decentral principle of internet/blockchain, that should give people (citizen,
employees and consumer) more power and freedom. Apart from this aspect, it is also
a question if blockchain developments are ready ‘yet’ for standardization? Therefore,
the management model and the infrastructure must probably be slightly further developed and (successfully) used.
The development of internet, as we know it today, did also not happen within a few
years. The same will apply for the development of standards for blockchain technology, because the interests are too big and we do not have a worldwide knowledge
network yet to quickly and professionally publish organizational problems, to exchange solutions and to reach a consensus. Compared to internet, we are now, in
2018, in a similar stage as the beginning of a standard for e-mail traffic, one of the
many parts of internet. One of the many initiatives that eventually led to the TCP/IP
protocol was Open System Interconnection (OSI). OSI started in 1977 and was an
attempt by ISO to realize a standard for a network of computers to exchange data.
But many saw the standard as too complex and inefficient to be used widely. There
was also resistance from existing suppliers who had already invested a lot in other
systems. The TCP/IP protocol is much more pragmatic and cheaper to realize.
Eventually, after about fifteen years, it has become the standard for data exchange
via internet.
120 Authors of the book: ‘Blockchain Revolution: How the Technology Behind Bitcoin is Chang-

ing Money, Business, and the World’ (2016).
121 More about this in the Weconomics analysis book, paragraph 6.6: ‘Internet of the people’.

Boek8_Kopie_Intern_Gebruik_Weconomics

129

3.7 Blockchain is industry agnostic
Agnostic, from an organizational model perspective, refers to a concept where data
is generalized in a way that it is operable among different information systems. The
term can also refer to IT-systems or more specific blockchain technology, blockchain
practices, processes and businesses. In our opinion Blockchain Organizing is industry agnostic if you design blockchain organizations with a common ontology, taxonomy and organizing fundamentals. Industry agnostic means that is basically doesn’t
matter in which (traditional) industry you want to apply Blockchain Organizing.
Blockchain is about transporting a value, translated in bits and bytes, from supply to
demand, from A to B with a consensus ‘Yes’ to these four and earlier mentioned
important and fundamental questions:
1. Are sender and receiver the persons who they claim they are?
2. Has the transport between supply and demand been executed safely?
3. Was the sender the owner of the value?
4. Did the sender spend the value only once?
Once there is consensus on the ‘Yes’ of these questions the transaction is valid and
processed into a block and a block into to a blockchain. If we reason from the
situation that every industry has transactions, a transaction is a value transport and
every value can be translated in zero’s and ones, all transaction can be designed in
the same way at the lowest levels of shared information and transaction networks. If
you digitalize values, for instance the claim that you are owner of a house, is a digital
paper stored at the land register. If you sell a house you will have a digital transport
from supply to demand. So selling a house could be seen as digital transport or
transport of a closed box. And it doesn’t matter what is in ‘the box’. A blockchain
doesn’t care. Transporting a value from supply to demand can be seen as transporting
a closed box from A to B. You could open the box and see a bitcoin, ten bitcoins or
the ownership of a house. Still a closed box will be transported from A to B. Money,
goods and services they all can be translated into bits and bytes and as a message be
transported from A to B. So Blockchain Organizing is about transporting bits and
bytes and these can represent all kinds of products and services from all kinds of
industry.

Boek8_Kopie_Intern_Gebruik_Weconomics

130

Figure 3.9: Blockchain is industry agnostic: it doesn’t care what’s in the box

Most of the companies or governments we as authors are asked to work with, use
their own business model, context, processes and their own industry as a starting
point to design and develop a new blockchain organization. This is understandable
but not necessary and ‘dangerous’. Dangerous in the sense that technology alone
won’t change enough to address the problems we are facing as a society and as mentioned in paragraph 4.1. We all witness an ‘explosion’ of possible use cases. Every
organization wants to be the first with a blockchain application and think they are
unique. Every industry and almost every organization wants to start a blockchain
project that starts from their own situation and context. And very soon they find
themselves in a situation whereby a disruptive technology has been integrated in an
old organization model: a new soul has been fitted in old body. When substantial
investments in a new technology have been made, over a five to ten year period you
are not going to change your strategy that easily. New technology will, again, be applied in outdated organization models with the sole intention to protect existing revenue models. And as a consequence, in ten years we all will conclude: blockchain is
a nice decentral technology, but it is still business as usual.
If we, together with a disruptive technology, don’t change the way of organizing
supply and demand, nothing will really change fundamentally. Just automate analogue processes won’t help us solve the trust and productivity paradox or the power
and privacy problem for example. When we analyze Blockchain Organizing with a
deductive approach you could say that:
1. Different industries process transactions (major premise)
2. All transactions are basically the same from a digital perspective (minor premise)
3. All industries are the same (conclusion from a transaction perspective)
An agnostic way of doing transactions within the concept of Blockchain Organizing
means that you execute transactions via different channels (see also figure 2.1 and
3.5), but with a common basic infrastructure. The supplier can choose an application
to profile and offer products and services and, connected to the same data common,
the customer can choose a channel to select, purchase and pay. Together with a smart
contract they can execute the transaction workflow because they both want to execute a valid transaction. Agnostic means: make your own content available through
every contribution and distribution method possible. The opposite of agnostic would
be a situation whereby every electronic device in your house or office needs a different type of power socket.
Blockchain is basically a simple model to transport a digital value from A to B with
only four conditions the network has to agree upon: authentication of sender and
receiver, safe transport without interference, was there an input reference and was
the value send only once. When all existing organizations and industries start to apply
blockchain from their context or perspective, the power of blockchain will soon

Boek8_Kopie_Intern_Gebruik_Weconomics

131

disappear and business as usual will take over (again). We hope that blockchain will
not enter history books as the simplest but at the same time the ‘most-complex-toimplement-innovation’ in decades.
Conclusion
It is still too early to come to a blockchain standard as part of Blockchain Organizing.
Developing standards before there is a common ground, or a joint understanding on
what blockchain exactly entails, what it could mean for us people and which problems it addresses, is not very smart. On the other hand there is already a lot of commonality and standardization in the blockchain protocol itself.
There is a large difference of opinion between, academia, organization designers and
software developers on the other hand and business leaders and commercial parties
who decide on investments and the development of consumer and business applications on the other hand. First we will have to learn and develop more use cases before
we take them into production. But before that we have to agree on the social problems we want to solve and de organizational model we are going to us for this. This
‘exercise’ does not only deal with the technical part, but also the:
▪ Economic aspect: how does blockchain smartly combine supply and demand and
what will this mean for the existing economic actors and business models?
▪ Political aspect: which powers, regulation and legislation will come into force if
blockchain is used in such a way that existing powers will lose their power basis?
▪ Legal aspect: what are the legal consequences of another management model to
organize supply and demand? What if there is a better balance between buyer and
seller due to better (more objective synchronous real-time) information exchange?
What is the need for contract in a new digital context?
And more widely, for example in the Weconomics philosophy, the following aspects
should also play a role:
▪ Ecological: does blockchain ensure that we will consume in a more sustainable
way and waste less?
▪ Psychological aspect, mainly identity: man shapes tools and then tools shape us.
Would we behave differently as a human being in our interactions, economic traffic, home situation if we have different tools to organize trust?
▪ Social aspect: will inequality reduce when more people have access to a general
data utility facility to which a blockchain infrastructure will be connected later on?
In addition to the importance of blockchain as technology, we mainly see blockchain
as ‘a way of organizing’. To understand this new way of organizing, we must first
find consensus on the problem we want to solve and the direction we want to go.
What is progress and how do we get there? Therefore, we will have to redesign the
existing systems with requirements, wishes and processes. So, before we develop
technology (standards) from the existing mind-set and system, we believe that we
must first contemplate the system itself. We will have to pay attention to the principles that we will use in further developments and use of the technology. Otherwise

Boek8_Kopie_Intern_Gebruik_Weconomics

132

blockchain will change as much or as little as internet (depending on your views and
interests).
Primarily, a tool like Blockchain Organizing should contribute to the solution (and
definitely not the creation) of social organizational problems. We will not benefit at
all if a blockchain standard must include exceptions for a specific domain or certain
sectors. It is not necessary at all to make a distinction between different domains
when you reason from similarities, foundations and smallest building blocks. Inherent to the existing software industry, is that they benefit from differences and complex systems. This has little to do with standardization and technology, more with
perspective and politics. It would be great if we could get ‘the industry’ interested in
a new target for organizational technologies that we develop: new organizational
technology and standards within, must contribute to the improvement of the meaning of human work, productivity, privacy and power for the citizen. As authors of
this book, we are happy to contribute to this endeavor.

Boek8_Kopie_Intern_Gebruik_Weconomics

133

4. What does it mean for your organization?
When we see Blockchain Organizing as a mechanism, ability, enabler or a tool to
manage transactions within an organization and especially between organizations,
there are many valorization or application options. Here are some examples of Blockchain Organizing:
▪ selecting, hiring and connecting humans and resources to tasks, projects and finalizations;
▪ organizing Employee Self Service whereby employees can profile, select, order,
pay and register HR instruments on a blockchain themselves;
▪ organizing trust within your organization, for example through identity management and document control;
▪ smart contracts for workflows, employment contracts, framework agreements;
▪ expense payments, payroll and personnel administration, personnel information
systems.
In general, you can apply Blockchain Organizing well in those domains with many
buyers and sellers (fat markets), little regulations, relatively expensive intermediaries
(such as temporary employment agencies, payroll companies and interim-management agencies) and enough innovative capacity. In this chapter, we start with the
introduction of some domains (industries) which are relevant for managers and their
consultants. We will mainly talk about organizing:
▪ money, trade and economy (trust and economics)
▪ HR and management domain (work)
▪ supply chains, cooperation between organizations (efficiency, transparency)
▪ government tasks (commonalities)
You might also expect an explanation of applying Blockchain Organizing for the
financial sector, also called Fintech, but there are already several specific books about
this. In addition, we currently have the feeling that in this sector, although it sounds
paradoxically because most investments are made here, the least will change fundamentally in the short run. This is mainly due to the fact that blockchain is an important mechanism to render many parts of the financial sector irrelevant. The reason that a lot of money and effort is being invested now has, in our opinion, mainly
to do with internal efficiency improvements and self-preservation. At the beginning
of 2018 we have the feeling that more people are involved in buying and selling
cryptocurrencies then actually designing and developing usefull ideas and applications to solve social problems.
Before we go into these four domains, we start with the question: which problems
can you solve with Blockchain Organizing?

Boek8_Kopie_Intern_Gebruik_Weconomics

134

4.1 Which problems can you solve with Blockchain Organizing?
The rise of most populist movements in Europe, at the beginning of the twenty-first
century, can largely be contributed to the fact that our welfare state has become too
expensive. The expectations cannot be met through sufficiently high productivity
growth. If we do not want to take on more debt, privatize care duties, pay tax, work
longer and still keep our welfare intact, we must become more productive122. Since
the 1980s the productivity growth has lagged behind the rapidly growing costs of
care, education, security and climate change. This is starting to become a worldwide
and fundamental problem, which unfortunately is acknowledged by few politicians,
policy maker and business leaders123.
One of the main challenges that we address with Blockchain Organizing, is the improvement of the productivity of information workers. We can easily switch to a 6hour working day. The technology, to realize this, is available. All we need now is the
(political) insight and the will to change. When you improve productivity, without
producing more or firing people, you save time. Blockchain Organizing can ensure
that you can do the same amount of work in less time. The surplus time is then
available for tasks that the government can no longer afford to pay for at the contemporary level, such as healthcare, education, safety and the consequences of climate change. Therefore, we can maintain the same welfare without additional costs.
Blockchain Organizing is a form of organizational technology. Organizational technology can help us become more productive provided we find a meaningful interpretation of the surplus time that will become available. Now we invent fakework at
the office to avoid real work where it is needed. Apart from productivity, there are
other problems that we can solve with organizational technology. In this book we
will mainly focus on the following challenges and drivers:
1. Purpose: why innovate (computer) machines and still want to work?
2. Productivity: improving the productivity of information workers
3. Privacy: stricter European laws for the protection of personal data
4. Power: becoming less dependent on capital, governments and tech companies
5. Propaganda: more towards a shared reality
6. Process: efficient performance of processes within existing organizations
Purpose
We require a new perspective related to the question: where are we going and how
do we get there? What is progress? When will our children be better off? More and
more people lose the direction, the purpose, the reason of living. It was always clear:
