you worked to provide for your family and to give your children a better life. That
was progress: making sure that your children would be better off and for example
could buy a house or car or complete a study. This form of progress is ‘bluntly’
122 Other alternatives are ‘making’ more children or allowing more immigrants.

123 See for example article in the Financial Times: ‘Weak productivity turns into a problem of

global proportions’ (25-05-2015).

Boek8_Kopie_Intern_Gebruik_Weconomics

135

translated into: economic growth. But (continuous) economic growth is not the same
as progress. In fact: it endangers progress. The sadness is that our own invention,
you could call it capitalism and within that neo-liberalism, will eventually kill us.
We see that the purpose in our lives is moving from more and more welfare to more
well-being. With this movement, the meaning of work and how we will organize ‘new
work’ also changes. For example, if you as government, the unemployment and benefit office, trade union or employers’ association are continuously engaged in the
employment market and want to give people the prospect of a permanent job, you
probably use the idea of having permanent jobs as the basis. It then becomes selfevident that creating employment and having a permanent job is desirable and good.
The idea that job creation is a good idea is framed: policymakers create the circumstances that their idea becomes true. The question is who benefits from this? The
people, the government or the institutions and companies that are engaged in employment services?
A permanent job is only a regulation principal to reduce insecurity in supply and
demand of work, something that provides ‘security’ of income or adds value. But we
know that is a ‘fake’ security to increase supply if there is no demand. Arrangements
such as permanent jobs, seems fine during the Industrial Revolution. However, if we
cannot meet the expectation of our welfare state with a higher productivity, hierarchies, permanent jobs and full employment can have a negative effect if used as regulation principle. More and more people are looking for the meaning of work and
employment organizations and can no longer find this in the answers of the establishment. The ‘basic income’ theme also continues to return when we talk for example about robotization and the disappearance of jobs.
When filling or organizing permanent jobs is your task, you probably no longer wonder about the question what a job actually is. Why there are jobs and why we actually
work (in an economic sense). The theme employment also works well during elections: nobody is in favor of more unemployment and nobody is against more jobs.
Right? We have become a supply economy. We have offices, we employ people and
to give them security, we subsequently start looking for finalizations because our
fixed costs still have to be paid.
That is the opposite world of how it should be. The effectiveness question is apparently no longer important. Why do we, people, innovate (computer) machines (we
suppose with the idea to take over our work) and at the same time we want to continue to work? Why do we strive for full employment? Why do computers not ensure
that we become more productive in offices so we have to work less and can spend
more time for example on education, care and leisure? We know that we do not have
enough time for the things that we find really important in our life. This means that
we must transfer time from non-productive working in offices, to a meaningful contribution to our direct environment and society.

Boek8_Kopie_Intern_Gebruik_Weconomics

136

Increasingly more people are critical regarding the perspective of full employment
and are looking for a new meaning and intention of work, employment organizations
and unemployment. Blockchain Organizing, in combination with a new perspective
on work and employment organizations, can ensure that we have more time available. In relation to the organization of work, Blockchain Organizing will lead to us
having to work less. We will not spend the surplus time on tasks that are irrelevant
as Cyril Parkinson124 noted (also called bullshit jobs125), or on producing things we
don’t need, but on sustainability.
If we move to programmable institutions and Decentralized Autonomous Organizations (DAO)126 with the use of Blockchain Organizing, we, as human beings, must
mainly and firstly think about what we will do with the spare time. Or as formulated
by Karl Marx: “The production of too many useful things results in too many useless people.”
People who no longer have to go to the office unnecessarily because smart machines,
computers and protocols have taken over the work, will have more spare time. They
can use this time to perform tasks that the government is handing back to the citizen127. This way we can slow down the crumbling welfare state and bring it back to
the old level.
Productivity
Since the 1970s (rise of offices) we see a drop in productivity growth. You could say
that the productivity growth, on average in Western countries in agriculture and industry is above the level of cost increases in welfare. The problem is in the productivity growth of information and knowledge worker (see also figure 4.1).
When we look at the figures128 of the development of productivity growth during
the period 1800-2010, we see the following picture:

124 See also book: ‘Parkinson's Law: The Pursuit of Progress’ (1955).

125 As these are called by David Graeber. David Graeber (1961- ) is an American anthropologist,

anarchist and professor of Social Anthropology at Goldsmith University of London. Graeber is
activist (Occupy Wall Street). His contract at Yale University would not have been renewed for
questionable reasons.
126 Decentral Self-Operating Organizations: similar to autonomous driven cars.
127 Consider the concrete interpretation of the participatory society where the central government
transfers tasks to councils and councils to the citizen. Enforces, because we, as citizens, do not
want to governments to borrow more money, to privatize more public facilities or to charge
more taxes.
128 England is the country where Industrial Revolution is first visible in the improvement of
productivity. In figure 4.1 we see the productivity growth since 1800. The figures are from the
Bank of England. See also website:
bankofengland.co.uk/research/Pages/onebank/threecenturies.aspx

Boek8_Kopie_Intern_Gebruik_Weconomics

137

Figure 4.1: Productivity growth all industries since 1800 (Jan-00 from left to right is: January
1800, January 1900 etcetera)

A drop in growth does not have to be a problem. It will only become a problem
when the welfare costs increase faster than productivity. After all you must be able
to justify more consumption with more production. Because our welfare costs increase faster than our productivity, a gap occurs that has mainly been filled with
privatization, borrowed money and more taxes, since the eighties. As a result, the
national debt in the Netherlands for example has increased from 86 billion euros in
1980 to more than 450 billion in 2016. After the crisis of 2008 we believed that
budget cuts would solve the problem, but that failed. The national debt has almost
doubled since the crisis.
The main and most fundamental way to balance production and consumption is the
improvement of productivity: as a person, you may spend more on welfare if you
produce more. But our productivity does not grow as much as our welfare costs.
Especially information workers become insufficiently more productive because they
use expensive industrial institutions to reduce insecurity, they always must retype the
same data and they find it difficult to share information because there are walls between departments, companies and systems. Partly because we still organize demand
and data in a traditional way, the productivity in offices does not grow sufficiently.
It is time that we start to use an ‘internet assembly-line’ in our digital society,
whereby values are automatically transferred from workstation to workstation.
Blockchain technology can provide this. Work and employment organizations must
fit together. They have not done so since the end of the twentieth century.

Boek8_Kopie_Intern_Gebruik_Weconomics

138

To meet the increasing demand for productivity growth, we must first switch to a
fundamentally other organizational design for our digital society. This new design
also has the advantage that it aligns better with the new and much stricter European
privacy laws.
Privacy
Everybody has to face it, only a few are prepared for it129. Since 2016 there is new
European legislation regarding the protection of personal data. The new privacy law
has an impact on both individuals as well as organizations, mainly within IT organizations. Each organization has a HRM, CRM or ERP system with personal data.
Different articles130, make it clear how naïvely many people handle their personal
data. As a result their insurance policy could be cancelled or a mortgage application
is refused inexplicably. They could be stopped at an airport because someone has
stolen their identity. When it concerns the use of personal data, the naivety of many
people can be compared with entering into more debts during the 2008 crisis.
Initially people often react dismissive when we invite them to provide input regarding
the consequences of the new privacy legislation. For them it is still too far away. Or
they do make it a priority, or, as with entering debts, that the government will keep
‘an eye on things’. Unfortunately, governments themselves are guilty of not handling
personal data carefully. At the same time the phenomenon emerges whereby tools
start influencing our behavior. That is actually the intention of tools or institutions:
they are there to reduce the insecurity in our behavior. Our behavior is not just
formed by laws and rules, but also by IT systems. We like ‘free’ social media, we use
it every day, we get used to it, but eventually we cannot do without. We must take
into account that man forms the tool and then the tool forms us. This means that if
we are not aware, tools will become more and more powerful and therefore reduce
the power of people.
In addition to citizens, organizations are not (yet) really impressed by the General Data
Protection Regulation (GDPR). Directors often haven not even heard of it. However, a
data leak that is reported too late can easily costs your organization up to four percent
of the worldwide annual turnover! And the risk of data leaks is ever increasing with
the complexity of IT systems that are continuously added with new functions. Plus,
more and more data is stored in ‘the cloud’ and more and more systems are connected to each other via internet. Lately a customer told us: “We have connected so many
IT systems that we did not even notice that there was a hack.”
Blockchain Organizing ensures, with new concepts like Personal Data Service (PDS),
Bring Your Own Data (BYOD) and Data Logistics, that companies and governments have to ‘own’ less data to perform their processes. Not ownership, but access
129 In 2016 a total of 5,500 data leaks were reported to the Data Protection Authority (DPA) in

The Netherlands. Most reports are from (healthcare) insurers, hospitals, banks and governments.
See also: https://autoriteitpersoonsgegevens.nl/en
130 See for example article from Jérôme Groetenbriel: 'Tinder must be joking with their “download your data” option' (October 31, 2017).

Boek8_Kopie_Intern_Gebruik_Weconomics

139

becomes important. So, you cannot leak data as an organization if you don’t have the
data in your own systems. For example, if a webshop requires your address to send
a product, they ‘ping’ a request to your PDS: What is your address? Subsequently
they receive a reply and as soon as the transaction is completed your address will no
longer be present in their system (unless you give explicit permission for this, but
why would you do that?).
By fundamentally changing the organization of (personal) data with blockchain technology, we also address the problem of the increasing power of large, often American, data companies.
Power
In Europe, there is a growing need to become less dependent on powerful technology companies131. Currently you as consumer do not have a real alternative for free
of charge platforms, where your personal data is the product that generates revenue.
The business model of most free (social media) platforms is simple: the platform is free
but you pay by giving your data. You pay through exchange: your data as raw material for
the production of adverts or datasets by Facebook, in exchange for free use of the
infrastructure.
It seems that we are not aware what it can mean to be dependent on a limited number
of companies from Silicon Valley. You can compare it with the dependence on cheap
oil supplied by a limited number of countries from the Middle East. If Europe wants
to become less dependent on American tech companies, it is important that there
will be a better cooperation to organize the supply and demand of data. Just as when
the borders were opened in 1992132, logistics, in this case data logistics, also plays an
important role again.
For many technical innovations, it is necessary that these are realized based on a
strong cooperation (co-creation) between knowledge institutions, enterprises, governments, interest organizations and users. Of course, we can wait till large tech companies come with a standardization of the blockchain protocol, but it is better to
contribute to this ourselves from a joint European cooperation, or maybe even stipulate it.
By further developing and applying innovations such as PDS, we can respond to the
undesired power growth, not just of American tech companies, but also of central
governments, e-commerce companies, banks and insurers. Related to the power of
large companies is the power of financial capital. The improvement of the productivity provides more advantage for money lenders than for human capital. Apart from
the lagging productivity growth, the available growth is disproportionately divided.
131 Also called the ‘big five’: Alphabet (Google), Amazon, Apple, Facebook and Microsoft.

132 See also: Bessems, P., R. Schenk & L Verspui (red.): “International distribution logistics after

1992”, final report Industria study trip 3-24 April 1989. TU-Eindhoven: Study association of the
Faculty of Business Administration (Dutch only).

Boek8_Kopie_Intern_Gebruik_Weconomics

140

Real incomes are lagging behind productivity growth. Much employment is replaced
by technology and mainly money lenders make money on this.
Propaganda
2016 was not just the year of Brexit, increasing populism and the victory of Donald
Trump, but also of the renewed attention to fake news and manipulated news. Fake
news is news of which the creator is consciously aware that the news is incorrect and
distributes this with a certain intention. Manipulated news does not necessarily have
to be true. For example, facts can be omitted or certain messages have a higher level
in search results or on timelines. Propaganda, that covers a part of the fake news and
manipulated news, has been around for some time, but it is becoming an increasingly
bigger problem through the intensive use of internet and social media.
For example, it cannot just influence elections, which it has always done, but maybe
even determine. By using propaganda in combination with social media, people with
more money can easier influence decisions than people with fewer resources. An
investor in Facebook will not directly influence the timeline algorithm, but definitely
indirectly. This process can undermine our democracy. Even one of Facebook early
investors and former ‘coach’ of Mark Zuckerberg worries about Facebook's algorithm and power133. Blockchain technology can help to slow this process down.
Internet critic Evgeny Morozov134 is right to indicate that technological progress
does not automatically lead to social progress. The modern world has perhaps become too complex to understand. This has everything to do with the aforementioned
trust paradox. With Blockchain Organizing a new way is created to address this. The
number of (technical) tools with which we can form, realize and control a shared
reality will improve. But whether we use technology properly is up to people. Because
despite all technological improvements, human contact and interaction still remains
the most important way to try to understand the complex world together in a dialogue, and to form a picture or opinion. Technology cannot replace all aspects of
human interaction.
Process
In the short term blockchain technology will help to ensure that processes within
organizations can be carried out more efficiently135. Especially within financial institutions, there will be more and more investments in blockchain applications, for example to make the payment traffic more efficient and quicker136. Banks also invest in
Fintech start-ups that can perform certain processes cheaper. But in the short term
133 See for example article from by Roger McNamee: ‘How to Fix Facebook - Before It Fixes Us’

(January 2018).
134 Evgeny Morozov wrote the book ‘The Net Delusion’, in which he shows that authoritarian
regimes use internet to suppress resistance.
135 Currently, 70 per cent of all financial transactions are executed via algorithms.
136 See for example report from Morten Bech and Rodney Garratt, employed at The Bank of International Settlements (BIS): ‘Central Bank cryptocurrencies’ (September 2017).

141

these Fintech companies often require a bank again because of the banking license.
That means an extra intermediary in the chain rather than fewer intermediaries. This
is at odds with the intention of Blockchain Organizing: reduction of the number of
intermediaries in order to reduce insecurity and to organize trust without increasing
bureaucracy and complexity. The improvement of existing processes is mainly useful
when the activities are aimed at ‘preparing’ processes, so they can later be transferred
to a new blockchain organization. You can read more about the transition of existing
organizations to new blockchain organizations in chapter 5.
So far, we have seen six challenges Blockchain Organizing can contribute to. Now
we continue with a number of applications of Blockchain Organizing subdivided into
four themes: economy, work, supply chains and government.
4.2 Blockchain for the organization of money, trade and economy
The basis for a smoothly running economy is trust. When trust is not, or insufficiently, present, we, as human beings, will invent all kinds of tools to reduce the
insecurity in transactions and to organize trust. We innovate the purchase order, the
invoice, the double bookkeeping and audit for companies. To be able to trade internationally, we have export credits, exchange rates, we conclude trade agreements and
we develop import charges if we are not confident that the other country does business in an honest way. To organize our welfare we must pay taxes and we receive
surcharges and grants, and everything is checked several times. When we have a financial crisis, like in 2008, this is not solved by thinking differently, but by installing
more rules for banks and financial institutions.
According to Nancy McKinstry, CEO of Wolters Kluwer, every year thousands of
new rules and procedures are added to the financial sector. And if the government
ratifies a new privacy law, it will soon generate tens of thousands of new jobs for
Data Protection Officers, all institutions that should reduce insecurity, but at the
same time increase the number of parties and therefore the complexity between supply and demand. We solve coordination problems with even more super specialists
and institutions that we subsequently badly coordinate. These are all examples of not
considering what is the most effective way to handle these issues and shows little
knowledge of the most basic organizational principles. Policy makers expect that
more rules and procedures (and therefore staff and management) will improve the
performance of transactions, while the opposite is the case.
The previously mentioned research by Hamel and Zanini shows that the costs of an
excessive bureaucracy in the United States constitute seventeen percent of the GDP.
The average span or control is 1:4.7, one manager/staff for almost five direct employees. In ‘lean’ organizations such as Morning Star and Svenska Handlesbanken
the ratio is 1:10. The main reason for a larger span or control is decentralization. The
figures in the US can be compared with other OECD countries. By using new coordination mechanisms, such as Blockchain Organizing, the productivity can grow

Boek8_Kopie_Intern_Gebruik_Weconomics

142

additional by 1.3% per year137 because we require less bureaucracy and institutions.
This growth would already suffice to keep up with the increasing welfare costs.
So, we must not contemplate how to regulate banks, but we must ask ourselves how
we can make banks less relevant by becoming less dependent on money as ‘medium
of exchange’ for transactions. By continuing to think in the same manner we did
before, we’ll try to solve new problems with the same frame of mind that created
them in the first place. Instead, we must think in a new way and towards a new system
that is less dependent on social institutions. We must switch to a system whereby
two parties (preferably two people), can do business with each other with as little
friction as possible. Blockchain Organizing can help with this by ‘programming’ institutions into smart contracts. These will execute transactions based on a technical
script, no longer a social script.
Various steps with transactions
When you buy something in a store, using your credit card, it often requires five to
seven parties to make sure the payment is executed correctly138. There are institutions
between the cash machine and bank account of the shopkeeper, that must reduce
the insecurity. After all, you do not want your money to go to the wrong party and
the shopkeeper wants to make sure that there are sufficient funds available. Trust is
fundamental for trade and economy. Without trust our community would not function. The only question is: What is the best, most efficient way to reduce insecurity
in transactions. The core of organizing trust in trade includes a number of parts:
1. The seller must proof: ownership of the value that is to be rented or sold, and the
right to rent out the property.
2. The seller can only spend a certain value or right once.
3. A party must be able to be authenticated, sometimes without revealing their identity.
4. The value must arrive at the receiver for whom it is intended.
5. A non-mutable registration of the transaction must take place.
6. It must be possible to securely execute the transaction.
To be able to perform these steps, costs have to be incurred. These costs are part of
the direct transaction costs. Apart from these direct transaction costs, there are also
costs to maintain the reputation of institutes or to maintain the trust in the money
system or currency. Money is still one of the main institutions to organize trust.
Money is a medium of exchange. It is an important tool, also called the lubrication
for our (open) economy139. Most money that we use is fiduciary money. This means:
money that you can trust as a citizen because the government monitors that it is and
remains legal tender. In the past, coins had an intrinsic value. The intrinsic value of
137 See also article by Gary Hamel and Michele Zanini: 'Excess Management Is Costing the U.S.

$3 Trillion Per Year', in Harvard Business Review (5-9-2016)
138 Also called clearance & settlement.
139 More about the role of money as currency, as medium of exchange in the Weconomics analysis book, paragraph 5.4: ‘Economy and the role of money’.

Boek8_Kopie_Intern_Gebruik_Weconomics

143

tangible money is the value of the material from which the coin is made. Currency
money consists of coins and notes held by the public. The nominal value of money
is the amount that is printed on the coin or note.
In addition to currency money, we have started to use more and more giro or digital
money. Here money actually only consists of zeros and ones in the accounting system
of banks and trust is even more important. When we use money as medium of exchange, and that definitely applies for digital money, you must be able to trust that
you can buy a comparable value with the received money. This means, for example,
that the government must assure that you can use money as general tender, that the
expected value doesn’t change too much, that the inflation does not get too high or
too low and that your savings are guaranteed up to a certain amount.
These are all tools to organize trust, to reduce insecurity and to enable transactions
so the economy is running smoothly. The trust in a good payment system with the
settlement of transactions is essential. The use of fiduciary money helps with this,
but it also has disadvantages such as speculation and the additional printing of money
without a real production or economy in return or without a democratic decision
made.
Your own medium of exchange for your community
A community can decide to use an own coin or token within the community as calculation unit to be able to exchange values more easily. In order to trust digital
money, the various parties of a community (some kind of mini-economy) must cooperate smartly140, harmonize processes and work from a joint ontology. They must
have a connection (see also figure 2.6: ten core connections of a community) and be
able to trust each other. The use of an own coin can help with this. In addition, it
should be possible to exchange the own coin against other currencies, like euros or
dollars. It is also important that a community ensures that they adhere to existing
standards141, so that transactions with other communities are also possible. When
this is met, Blockchain Organizing can ensure that the number of intermediaries and
human activities can be reduced.
The exchange of values, without having to use money as medium of exchange, look
like an important way to bypass the existing powers and regulations. You only use a
calculation unit. The disadvantage of this solution is that it requires more energy in
clarifying values at the supply and demand site of the (possible) transaction. This is
necessary to quickly, easily and reliably find values to trade with. In the past, it was
easy on physical markets to exchange, for example, a bag of potatoes for a kilo of
cotton. Markets were clearly and physically visible. But now we have to deal with
worldwide trade on the one hand, what makes organizing trust more difficult, and
on the other hand we have internet to better match supply and demand. Now there
140 See for example: Dutch Payment Association that organizes the collective tasks in the national

payment system for its members, https://www.betaalvereniging.nl/en/
141 For example SEPA: Single Euro Payments Area.

Boek8_Kopie_Intern_Gebruik_Weconomics

144

is also Blockchain Organizing, so we can make transactions without having to trust
each other. Therefore, we must switch to another organizational model.
Blockchain Organizing looks at organizing demand and supply of money, from several perspectives, other than only making the process more efficient, which is done
by many banks and fintech start-ups for example. Blockchain Organizing also looks
at the effectiveness question: do we really need money as medium of exchange?
Blockchain ensures that there are also other ways of exchanging values. You do not
always need money as medium of exchange. This means that we become less dependent on existing and mostly centralized institutions in the payment system.
Money has many advantages, but also disadvantages. So, we have developed all kinds
of financial products, not with the purpose to reduce insecurity but to get a higher
yield. Making money with money is of course a really stupid idea in the long run. A
relationship must be retained between the money supply and real value. Financial
products have become so complex that nobody can properly assess the risks anymore, which increase the insecurity. Blockchain Organizing, in relation to money and
economy, is about designing a new ‘welfare machine’, that is less dependent on
money and institutions, to organize demand and supply.
Apart from organizing trust and economy, Blockchain Organizing can make an important contribution to organizing work, also an important pillar of our welfare.
4.3 Organizing work
Blockchain Organizing can make an important contribution to the productivity of
information workers, provided it is used with a new management model. When organizing work (the HR and management domain), it is important to bring supply and
demand together with as little friction as possible. For example: bringing the demand
of a job applicant together with the supply of a vacancy. The tool that does this at
the lowest costs, will eventually survive. Blockchain Organizing enables us to connect
people and resources to an objective of an organization, at minimum costs. Let’s
look at one example. Suppose you are a consultancy firm. You can define an innovation project to develop blockchain advice services. Because you have permanent
employees, you want to quickly employ these people in the development of the new
service provision. Therefore, you ignore the question whether these people are the
most suitable? Perhaps you could better engage external experts, but this involves
high transaction costs to find and to engage these.
Blockchain Organizing is a form of organizational technology, also called Orgtech.
For example, you could see Orgtech as ‘mother’ of Fintech (financial sector), HRtech
(Human Resource) and GovTech (government). Blockchain Organizing is a combination of a new organizational model (secondary), with a technique (tertiary) that
focuses on the improvement of productivity (primary). Organizational technology is
a concept that is primarily based on an organizational or management problem, that

Boek8_Kopie_Intern_Gebruik_Weconomics

145

develops a suitable organizational design with this and only then looks at a suitable
implementation technique or tool. Contrary to many hyped tools such as Telecommuting, Agile Working and Cloud Computing, organizational technology is not
based on the tool, but on the problem to solve and the design with which you coordinate supply and demand.
Organizational technology answers the question how you can organize as efficiently
as possible with the use of technology. It is an applied science, or field of expertise,
that is part of (or rather should be) Organizational Science142. Organizational technology should be a field of expertise that provides principles, foundations and recipes
to managers, consultants, trainers and blockchain designers and developers. Organizational technology should be a field of expertise that mainly promotes the cooperation between education and research on the one hand, and practice on the other.
This practice mainly consists of knowledge-intensive organizations, where relatively
many information or knowledge workers are used, where there are relatively many
investments in digitization, but where the productivity increase does not keep pace
with these costs.
We saw earlier that the transaction costs in a digital economy almost go to zero, so
it becomes more attractive not to perform activities yourself but to outsource these
to suppliers. It is even better to use smart networks, with smart contracts or agreements between the various stakeholders in the ecosystem. If activities in the ecosystem are a routine, occur frequently and fundamentally consist of IFTTT actions, the
network of nodes could develop into a Decentralized Autonomous Organization. In
the Netherlands, most people working in organizations currently mainly process data
into information and information into knowledge. The costs for finding, selecting,
storing, processing and sending data is almost zero. This creates partnerships that in
the past would mainly take place within the walls of a company, but can now be more
and more organized within blockchain communities. Using organizational technology makes the firm and traditional markets less and less attractive as ideal form to
prepare, execute and register transactions.
Until recently, when you googled ‘HR’, ‘HRM’ or ‘management’ in combination with
‘blockchain’, you would only get little to no relevant results. This is changing quickly.
It is expected that Blockchain will bring a similar change in our life as internet, but
also for the organization of work. After the mainframe, the PC, internet and social/mobile, we now see the next disruptive innovation: blockchain. The question is
now: ‘When will HR and management use blockchain technology?’ Large ICT companies, banks and insurance companies already invest billions in blockchain technology and the legal domain is also becoming more and more active. So, when will HR
and management follow?

142 See also the Weconomics theory book, paragraph 3.6: ‘Different disciplines of organizational

science’.

Boek8_Kopie_Intern_Gebruik_Weconomics

146

In our view and experience as blockchain consultants, HR and management are
mainly engaged in finding and connection people/resources to tasks, projects and
targets. At this moment, there is technology that is so powerful that it will not only
make the existing way of finding and connecting redundant, but even has the potential to make a large part of the activities of the default positions and organizations,
that are currently engaged in this, irrelevant! Imagine that you ignore this, because it
seems too difficult, too technical or too far away yet. Where will you stand after ten
years when blockchain technology has indeed undergone the same development as
internet? You will not directly notice it when you are too late.
Changes happen incremental, but when you compare the way we organized music or
film distribution now, with ten years ago, the change is revolutionary. From the examples of organizations like Blockbuster or Freerecordshop we see that this only
leads to problems many years later. But then it is often too late to adjust. If your
organization uses Blockchain Organizing too late, it will for example be more difficult to find and hire the best talents, because they do increasingly work with modern
organizational technology and are less and less willing to work in traditional companies.
For the HR and management domain it is also important to delve into Blockchain
Organizing, so they can assess what they can and cannot do with it. Blockchain Organizing is not technical or difficult. It is a concept that can be used by most professionals if they want this. However, a condition is that you dare to let go of your old
self-evidence and that you are open to a new perspective on organizing, so you will
continue to provide added value in a world of a new way of organizing and new
organizational technology.
What does blockchain mean for the organization of work?
Blockchain literally means a chain of blocks, whereby a block is created from a number of transactions. A transaction can also be the purchase of a management book
or training course and the performance of a project as portfolio worker. When you
‘connect’ all transactions of a person in time together, a chain is created of connected
tools to a person and of supply to demand. We also call this an e-portfolio. It is a
sequence of controlled and non-mutable transactions that the community has consensus on. As indicated before, you can see blockchain as an additional layer on top
of internet. This layer ensures that we cannot only exchange information between
sender and receiver in a secure and reliable way, but also make transactions.
Now many HR professionals and managers will say: But we have been making transactions via the internet for quite some time? That is correct. You probably have registered yourself online for a course or ordered a management book at Amazon. But
at this moment you order via the website of the seller, while you as buyer, as employee or as citizen would like to order via an own application, so you do not continuously have to enter the same data and your data is secure. Thereby it would be

Boek8_Kopie_Intern_Gebruik_Weconomics

147

handy if the creation of a purchase order and the payment would more or less go
automatically via a protocol.
The large difference between the current way of working and Blockchain Organizing
is that we in principle no longer require training institutes, publishers, payroll companies and temporary employment agency for the preparation and execution of
transactions. We also no longer require an administration to create purchase orders,
to place orders, to check invoices and to make payments. We do not even require a
bank for the payments. Also, employment contracts, whether or not for a permanent
job, fixed or flex organizing, assessments, etcetera, they all get a different meaning
or they are no longer required when you use a smart contract which is based on the
concept of Blockchain Organizing. In its core, organizing becomes switching connections on and off so transactions can be executed almost fully automatically.
This entails quite a bit. Blockchain Organization therefore has the potential to make
many (administrative) activities within the HR and management domain redundant.
Even the firm as default organizational form is largely no longer required. Because
the HR department, the manager and the company belong together, mainly traditional companies with their own HR departments and traditional managers will
change or disappear. Many departments still lean heavily on existing analogue processes that are digitized. But we will more and more organize the finding and binding
of resources with Decentralized Autonomous Organizations. Self-operating processes in a shared transaction network, similar to self-driving cars. So, it is important
for you as HR professional and manager to delve into Blockchain Organizing.
Blockchain Organizing integrates concepts and developments like Personal Data Service for employees, peer-to-peer platform such as vacancy databases, sharing
knowledge via communities, self-organizing capacity, digitization, crowdsourcing,
cloud computing, social media, open source and open innovation. Blockchain Organizing provides a new perspective on concepts like more autonomy, self-organizing capacity, more inspiration, decentralization, accountability, trust, value, trade,
leadership and entrepreneurship. It ensures that you can cooperate smartly with both
your colleagues as well as your environment (portfolio workers, customers, suppliers,
talents, etcetera.) with the same organization. It provides an effective policy related
to the organization of a fixed-flex shell.
Blockchain Organizing is mainly suitable for digital production and consumption and
will make information and knowledge workers much more productive, flexible and
innovative. This way we can strengthen our competitive position in the future and
organize a sustainable welfare. For example, the whole discussion about replacing
permanent jobs for flex contracts would not be any problem within Blockchain Organizing, because it uses smart contracts143, prior consensus and reputation management. A fixed or flex contract within Blockchain Organizing is nothing more and
143 See also for example Ethereum: A decentralized software platform (for smart contracts).

Boek8_Kopie_Intern_Gebruik_Weconomics

148

nothing less than a type of connection. This way you connect people and resources
to each other to realize an objective that you cannot realize as an individual entrepreneur.
Why should HR and management pay attention?
Organizations in the service and knowledge domain have a growing need to cooperate in a smarter way, not just internally, but mainly with their environment and with
professionals such as portfolio workers. This environment does not only include
customers, competitors and suppliers, but also talents, trainers, temporary employment agencies, portfolio workers, governments and interest organizations. The necessity to cooperate in a smart way is only growing because we must become more
productive due to aging and increasing welfare costs.
The environment is becoming increasingly bigger, more dynamic and more complex.
Old rigid forms no longer work. More and more organizations are therefore looking
for new models and organizational forms. HR professionals and managers, who were
already working in the nineties, can still remember the question that many organizations were facing that time: ‘Must we join internet or not?’ Must we put our vacancies
on line? Must we put our manual and training offers on intranet? And when will we
switch to e-HRM and Employee Self Service? It is hard to imagine these questions
now. But still, in the coming years, HR professionals and managers will have to answer a similar question: Should we switch to a blockchain or not?
To be able to answer this question correctly, it is useful that you first delve into
Blockchain Organizing. Because designing, setting up and managing blockchain organizations is not a daily job for most people, and requires a lot of expertise, more
and more often blockchain professionals are also deployed within the HR and management domain. An expert in Blockchain Organizing, for example, is competent in
devising, designing, setting up, managing and implementing coordinating capacity
that uses blockchain technology. A good design is also important for this (especially)
within the HR and management domain to prevent problems in the implementation
later on. The better the design, the less management and HR is needed in the implementation and exploitation phase. Blockchain Organizing for example means that
you already consider during the design, that you will cooperate more and more with
your environment and less and less with your ‘regular’ colleagues. It does not only
consider the smallest building blocks and the connections between them: Blockchain
Organizing takes this as a fundamental principle.
Blockchain application for HR and management
For the HR and management domain many applications of Blockchain Organizing
can be imagined. Everything that in essence can be reduced to the smallest building
blocks and the connections between them and the connection of these capacities to
a finalization can be organized with blockchain principles. When discussing a number
of examples, it is important to mention that there is not just one blockchain, not even
for the HR and management domain.

Boek8_Kopie_Intern_Gebruik_Weconomics

149

Just like internet, an organization can for example opt for a public internet website,
an internet website with login and an intranet website. As organization you can
choose to join a public blockchain, but you can also decide on having your own
private or permissioned blockchain. Hybrid forms are also possible and more likely.
What is wise and practical, will differ per situation (see also paragraph 3.3). A few
examples of blockchain applications for the HR and management domain are:
▪ Identity, assigning and assuring; within HR and management it is important to be
able to establish the identity of an employee or flex worker and to monitor this.
With Blockchain Organizing you only have to do this once and you can use existing
identity systems.
▪ Purchase, searching and booking products and services: for example, searching and
booking training courses whereby the seller only has to keep track of training information at one location and the buyer can search and book a training course with
an own portal and workflow.
▪ Expat, requesting fiscal advice, residence permit and relocation: expats must organize a lot when they go abroad. This can be done in an easy, secure and efficient
way via Blockchain Organizing.
▪ E-portfolio, for work and lifelong education: when you have followed a training
course, finished a task or project, you receive feedback in your portfolio in a secure,
reliable and meaningful way. Once approved and recorded in your portfolio, you
can no longer change this yourself without creating a new transaction.
▪ (Employment) contracts, drawing up, distributing and storing: HR and management are often engaged in drawing up, distributing and monitoring contracts. This
can be easily realized with smart contracts. An employment contract actually includes a number of conditional actions, so it can be programmed.
▪ Payrolling: with payrolling, transactions are continuously executed via a certain
script between sender (payroll company) and receiver (employee). Within Blockchain Organizing there are no restrictions to the form or conditions of a contract.
Everything that can be included in an algorithm, can be organized via a blockchain.
The payroll company becomes redundant for the execution of transactions.
▪ Taxes, surcharges and grants: as employer you are responsible for paying premiums
to the Tax office, education fund and pension fund for example. This can easily be
done via Blockchain Organizing.
▪ Communication: HR and management communicate often with employees via all
kinds of media. A document or a message is also a tool that exists, has value and
with which you can make transactions. With a blockchain you can easily organize
this whereby you, regardless of the medium (WhatsApp, website, Twitter, e-mail,
etcetera), only have to keep track of your source at one location and the receiver
can read your message with an own application, in a reliable, secure and meaningful
way.
Which tasks will change or disappear?
Blockchain Organizing provides transparency and offers reliable, meaningful and
well secured data. Many routine and mechanical tasks and processes will be

Boek8_Kopie_Intern_Gebruik_Weconomics

150

programmed in a smart contract and performed by a protocol. Whether party A will
make a transaction with party B and how this will be organized with a blockchain,
will also depend on the reputation of both parties. The reputation can be traced back
to previous transactions and is confined in a blockchain. When a transaction is made,
the chain will be further complemented and therefore also the reputation of party A
and B and therefore also the reliability of the entire blockchain.
An e-portfolio, for example, is a collection of training courses, tasks and projects that
are connected to a person. When you book a course with a training institute, it will
be approved a third party and your supervisor and trainer will provide you with feedback in your own blockchain (e-portfolio). When adding content to your e-portfolio,
you could see this as a (data) transaction with a sender and receiver. Both parties 144
must agree on the question: is this data:
▪ always public
▪ never public
▪ public on request
In the last case both parties must agree on this question: To access the data you must
have:
▪ the key of the sender
▪ the key of the receiver
▪ both keys (also called a pair)
So, values are added to your portfolio that the community has reached consensus on.
In this way, others can make a better decision when executing a next transaction (for
example starting a new project). We can better connect specializations (supply) to
finalizations (demand) without increasing the complexity. The e-portfolio as an expression of Blockchain Organizing can be seen as coordinating mechanism to bring
demand and supply together in a smarter way. Smarter than your LinkedIn profile,
where you can basically post anything, without anybody checking this. Plus, a blockchain will run the entire ledger, from the moment that you started to bring your eportfolio on a blockchain. A date and time stamp are set per transaction, and the
public address of buyer, seller and product are known. We must take into account
that transactions will not only be executed with money as medium of exchange but
also with other forms with value, such as hours, credits, rights, energy, waste, other
products, etcetera. This offers, for example, options to convert a part of your salary
into additional free hours or parental leave without any problems. For example, you
can program that if an irregularity surcharge exceeds €1,000 the surplus must be converted into free hours at a valuation of €50 gross per hour.
Finally

144 This system could also be applied to health records for example where general practitioner

and patient must agree on data access.

Boek8_Kopie_Intern_Gebruik_Weconomics

151

HR, management and technique do not always have a frictionless relationship. But
you don’t have to be familiar with how the TCP/IP protocol works to be able to put
the staff manual on intranet. There is at least the same need, if not more, for people
who know how to design future organizations or use Blockchain Organizing meaningfully for the organization of work, than for people who can program a blockchain
application145. Because using new techniques for the solution of non-problems,
doesn’t make any sense.
4.4 Organizing supply chains
From the eighties onwards, more and more work has been outsourced, mainly due
to Japanese companies. This leads to so-called supply chains. When a company no
longer performs a certain process itself, this process is outsourced to a supplier. This
supplier is also an organization (factory) that probably performs the process in a
similar way, but usually much cheaper, because it produces a larger volume and it is
probably specialized in a certain process. When outsourcing work to a supplier, an
additional relationship, an additional connection is added between outsourcer and
supplier and therefore also additional transactions.
It is especially important to investigate this new relationship. Because if the supplier
must work precisely under the (transaction) conditions and must connect with the
systems of the outsourcer, the transactions costs across the entire chain will increase.
We saw before that a bilateral relationship (1:1) or an outsourcer with several suppliers (1:n) is not the same as a business ecosystem (n:n). Supply chains usually consist
of several 1:1 or 1:n intermediaries between the first seller and final buyer. This is
not the same as a business ecosystem. The latter is more based on equal relationships
between actors that use a smart contract and shared transaction network connected
to a data utility facility.
Marco Iansiti146, among others, professor at Harvard Business School, has done research on business ecosystems. Iansiti has contributed to the development of insights
related to the development of ecosystems and the relationship between organizations
and their ecosystem. This field of expertise is also called Ecosystem Strategy. According to him, the success of companies likes Walmart (and according to us at
ASML in the Netherlands) mainly leans on developing and managing a complex and
extensive ecosystem. He formulates this as follows147: “Many industries today behave like
a massively interconnected network of organizations, technologies, consumers and products. Perhaps
the most dramatic and widely known example is the computing industry. In contrast with the vertically integrated environment of the 1960s and 1970s, today’s industry is divided into a large number
145 A lot of blockchain code is open source and can for example be found on websites like

GitHub.
146 Marco Iansiti is mainly engaged in research on the interface between technology, operation
and innovation management.
147 See also his paper, together with Roy Levien (NERA): ‘Keystones and Dominators: Framing
Operating and Technology Strategy in a Business Ecosystem’ (24-2-2004).

Boek8_Kopie_Intern_Gebruik_Weconomics

152

of segments producing specialized products, technologies and services. The degree of interaction between
firms in the industry is truly astounding, with hundreds of organizations frequently involved in the
design, production, distribution, or implementation of even a single product. And because of this
increasingly distributed industry structure, the focus of competition is shifting away from the management of internal resources, to the management and influence of assets that are outside the direct
ownership and control of the firm.”
Because the bureaucracy in chains with 1:1 and 1:n relationships can quickly increase
(see also Hamel and Zanini research) we see that supply chains are growing towards
business ecosystems based on n:n relationships. Blockchain Organizing will play an
important role in this. Currently, especially the financial sector invests a lot in blockchain technology. There are probably even more opportunities in the Supply Chain
Management and logistical domain, also called SCM. Not in the least because SCM
is actually already a chain of transactions. In principle processes take place within an
institute (intra-firm), between institutes (extra-firm) and in a chain, supply chain or
shared transaction network (more firms).
Supply chain in the food chain
More than a hundred years separate a more agriculture-based society and our present
one. More than hundred years ago a large majority of the people was still working in
agriculture while production and consumption took place locally without intermediaries. Now a chicken breast ‘flies’ across the entire world, before it arrives on your
plate at home. The main reason for this is making processes more efficient through
specialization and scaling. Each actor in the chain adds a little value, but indeed with
a large volume. Blockchain Organizing has other principles, than just improving the
efficiency in existing organizations. Blockchain Organizing also focuses on the
productivity demand at macro level. To realize sufficient productivity growth, it is
important that final demand and supply are correctly aligned and to keep the aggregated transactions costs low.
In SCM we explicitly see the rise of institutions to reduce insecurity, with as a result
that the ‘chain’ becomes more complex. This is definitely the case in the food chain.
It is no surprise that Blockchain Organizing will find more and more applications in
this domain. Walmart, for instance, the largest retailer in the world, uses blockchain
technology for tracing & tracking pork meat from China148.
Another project is the chain of packed fruit and vegetables that is produced in the
US. With the use of blockchain technology and an online accounting system, the
identity and processes of a large number of parties, in these often complex supply
chains, can be better monitored. The main purpose is to make the food supply safer.
There are producers, slaughterhouses, transporters, freight forwarders, inspectors,
traders and consumers in the food chain. In addition, standardization, normalization,
148 See for example article in The Wall Street Journal: ‘Walmart Turns To Blockchain For Track-

ing Pork In China’ (19-10-2016).

Boek8_Kopie_Intern_Gebruik_Weconomics

153

software, research, education, legislation, etcetera is used. A large number of actors
that must cooperate smartly but that do not use the right tools for this.
Together with IBM and the Tsinghua University from Beijing, Walmart is building a
system to verify the identity of these parties and subsequently connect these. Transactions are made and logged with this system, without being able to adjust these
afterwards. Because supply chains in the food industry are long and complex, the risk
of fraud increases. Worldwide149, but also in the Netherlands, several fraud cases150
are known.
Besides fraud, food safety is also an important aspect. A study by Robert Scharff,
associated professor at the Ohio State University, estimates the annual costs for food
related consequences at 55 billion dollars for the economy in the US151. For example,
when food poisoning152 occurs, it must be possible to quickly identify the source.
Blockchain Organizing also offers advantages for the ‘speed’ aspect. Because ‘input
reference’ is a basis of blockchain technology, it is possible to quickly identify the
previous party.
Trust and insecurity reduction are an important aspect within the food chain. As we
saw before, the core of Blockchain Organizing is the reduction of insecurity, not by
adding even more intermediaries in the supply chain, but by programming insecurity
reduction in a (blockchain) protocol. This does not increase the complexity, unless
we also strive for full employment besides (blockchain) innovation.
Other chains
Apart from the food chain, it is also important to know that clothes are not made
with the use of child labor, and factories are safe. Remember the reports of fires in
unsafe clothing factories in Bangladesh for instance. For the organization of a sustainable welfare, it is important to invest in sustainably organizing chains. Consumers, governments as well as interest organizations have an increasing need for information about the way products are made and to what extent this is done, for example,
socially and climate- and energy-neutral. Generally, actors in a chain have no interest
in making this information transparent. But due to social pressure and technological
progress, we can make supply chains much more transparent and gain trust without
additional intermediaries.
Using Blockchain Organizing within the SCM domain provides the option to identify
and monitor entities in the chain from source to consumption. Tracing & tracking is
149 See for example: https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2008_Chinese_milk_scandal

150 See for example the fipronil fraud in the egg sector in The Netherlands (2017).

people therefore end up in hospitals and 3,000 people die.

154

a relatively old concept within SCM, but now gets new impetus through improved
technology. It offers the opportunity to execute transactions at minimum costs and
there is a shared reality about the status of a process or the question where a product
originates from.
We previously indicated the importance of Data Logistics. SCM, logistics and Data
Logistics are very much connected to each other. They are not only an important
application area, but also a basis for Blockchain Organizing.
Examples
In addition to Blockchain Organizing for food and clothing chains, there are other
examples of applications for the SCM domain:
▪ Toyota Motor Corporation: wants to organize the tracking of car parts with blockchain technology.
▪ Everledger: is an initiative to trace the origin of diamonds, especially to counteract
the trade in blood diamonds.
▪ Provenance153: organizes supply chain certification (digital passport) on a blockchain. The passport guarantees authenticity (is the owner the person he claims to
be) and proven origin (input reference, where does the product come from?). A
digital product passport is a kind of Bill of Materials (BOM) that indicates from
which parts make up a completed product. A part is a value (asset) and the origin
of this value is transparent.
▪ Port of Rotterdam: a partnership of, among others, the Port of Rotterdam, ABN
Amro and the Technical University of Delft. They conduct research into the possible applications for, and later maybe the development of, blockchain technology.
The research mainly focuses on sharing logistical information and contract information154.
Advantages
Using Blockchain Organizing within SCM provides advantages such as:
▪ Real time smart data: quicker and more targeted data from a shared reality. Identifying the source (traceability) can for example be reduced from days to minutes.
▪ Fraud: becomes more difficult because the accounting system is shared, cannot be
changed and can be checked by the participating actors.
▪ Tracing & tracking: where are goods in the supply chain, where do they come
from, how many intermediaries are used etcetera.
▪ Costs: the transaction costs are reduced because more and more intermediaries
(institutions) are programmed in a blockchain protocol.
▪ Structured flexibility: using blockchain technology in a supply chain with many
actors makes it possible to organize tools (such as software and shared databases)

153 See also website: provenance.org

154 See for example article ABN-Amro: ‘Logistics sector gets to grips with blockchain technology’

(16-11-2016).

Boek8_Kopie_Intern_Gebruik_Weconomics

155

in a scalable way, while through scripts the differences between actors can be respected and people remain deployable in a flexible way.
So far, using Blockchain Organizing in supply chains. Now we continue with the use
of Blockchain Organizing for the organization and performance of government tasks
(regardless the question, what we, as democracy, believe what government tasks
should be).
4.5 Organizing government tasks
Governments can play an active role in the further development of blockchain technology and Blockchain Organizing. Not just as early adaptors for their own use, but
also a director and facilitator for other blockchain projects155, and mainly as initiators
of a general data utility facility. A number of practical effects of the role could be:
▪ stimulating and facilitating more research and education;
▪ taking the lead in the construction of a general data utility facility156;
▪ stimulating community working with shared information and transaction networks;
▪ active role with standardization of an ontology, taxonomy, and technology based
on new thinking and a new organizational model;
▪ using Blockchain Organizing with internal and external transactions.
For the government, we see a large number of applications. According to the aforementioned Agora model we live in four atmospheres: personal, private, public (government) and politics. Both citizens and companies can influence the public atmosphere via the political atmosphere. A government authority itself has both relationships with citizens and companies, as well as with own employees and other institutions. This provides a large number of transaction options that can be organized
smarter with Blockchain Organizing,
A few examples:
▪ Identity: creating an identity by linking an identity tool (for example a passport or
fingerprint), to a citizen. There is consensus about this link, that can be included
in a blockchain and no longer be changed or connected to another person afterwards. After all, you can only spend (read connect) a value once.
▪ Voting: a ballot paper is sent via a blockchain infrastructure and can only be used
once to cast a vote (value) by the person with the right key of the (virtual) safe
that contains the ballot paper. In exchange for a ballot paper, maximum one vote
can be cast (counter-value). Ballot paper and vote are logged in a blockchain, can
easily and reliably be counted and cannot be changed

155 See for example Amendment 34550-XIII-18, about stimulating blockchain technology by the

Dutch government by D66 MP Kees Verhoeven and VVD MP Erik Ziengs (27-10-2016).
156 See for example the ‘Discipl’ project from ICTU.

Boek8_Kopie_Intern_Gebruik_Weconomics

156

▪ Permit: requesting a permit includes a number of processes. This workflow must
be monitored so certain handling times are not exceeded. In addition, it must be
possible to make the different steps, considerations and decisions (all values)
transparent. The permit can be paid with digital currency for example, and the
permit can be connected to grants via a smart contract. This means that a company
only receives a certain grant if it also has the right permit.
▪ Implementing bodies: many administrative and executing bodies of the government such as land registry and the tax office make numerous transactions every
day. Requests, decisions and payments must be executed very efficiently. Subsequently the agreement must be performed via an IFTTT statement, whereby it is
important that the payments are made to the right person. In addition, it can be
programmed in a study allowance agreement, that the allowance can only be used
for payment to educational institutions.
▪ Document control: the government develops and controls many documents.
These must be controlled in a systematic, secure and meaningful way. This can be
done by considering a document as a value and by including this in a blockchain.
▪ Employment organization: the government is also an employment organization
where, for example, the e-portfolio of civil servants can be organized on a blockchain. Each time that a civil servant follows a course, does a project or changes
position, this is recorded in an e-portfolio. A group of connected portfolios is the
new personnel information system
Giving the many applications, it is clear that Blockchain Organizing is also relevant
for the government. But also for the government it applies that blockchain technology is not decisive, but the combination of technology with a different way of thinking and a fundamentally different organizational model for the coordination of demand and supply, is.
Advantages Blockchain Organizing for the government
Blockchain Organizing provides many advantages for the government and therefore
also for citizens. The large ‘disadvantage’ for the government is that Blockchain Organizing requires a lot less government. The question here is again: what will we do
with all these people who currently work for the government? Will we dismiss half
of them or will everyone who works 8 hours per day go back to a 4-hours working
day if technology doubles our productivity? In any case, Blockchain Organizing also
offers for the government an answer to the challenges of the 6 Ps: : purpose, productivity, privacy, power, propaganda and processes. The advantages of Blockchain Organizing for the government are:
▪ A permit request can be processed quicker without requiring personal data of the
requestor.
▪ The improvement of lead time, price and quality of identity products like passports.
▪ The improvement of transparency without additional costs. Policy, memos,
minutes, e-mail, and decisions: everything with value can be included in a blockchain, cannot be changed and can be made transparent.

Boek8_Kopie_Intern_Gebruik_Weconomics

157

▪ Participation-community gets content: returning more tasks to the private atmosphere by offering citizens and organizations a blockchain structure so that older
people for example can find care providers themselves and make transactions.
▪ Only having to maintain personal data at one location and to make this available,
for example, via a Personal Data Service, to buyers who require this data to perform processes.
▪ Designing, developing, implementing and checking new laws, permits and regulations can be done quicker and better by putting these in a smart contract. For
example, a farmer can automatically get a permit for a maximum expansion of X,
when a similar farmer discontinues his company within a radius of Y kilometers.
▪ The improvement of the trust in the government of citizens and companies by
making data meaningful, reliable and more secure with the use of Blockchain Organizing.
▪ The easier, quicker, less complex and cheaper development of applications that
connect to a general data utility facility.
So far, a more detailed explanation of a number of application options of Blockchain
Organizing and the problems that can be solved with this. In the next chapter, we
continue with the question how you can start with Blockchain Organizing within
your organization/network and what you require for this.
